<meta>This page serves as the entry point to the manuscript, documenting the physical state and markings of the front cover. The initials suggest a personal or library identification.</meta>

*The leather binding of this volume is heavily worn, showing significant scuffing and loss of material along the spine and corners, a testament to its age and handling over the centuries.*

*The front cover is made of dark brown leather, now mottled and aged. Centered near the top, the initials **C G H** are blind-stamped into the leather—a technique where letters are pressed into the surface without using gold or ink. Each letter is separated and flanked by small, six-petaled floral or star-shaped decorative stamps.*

# C G H

<note>These initials likely identify the original owner or the person who commissioned the binding.</note>

<term>Binding</term>: The protective cover and structure of the book.
<term>Leather-bound</term>: A book covered in animal skin, traditionally used for durability.
<term>Blind-stamped</term>: <note>Decorative impressions made in leather using a hot metal tool without adding gold leaf or color, leaving a subtle, recessed design.</note>
<term>Initials</term>: The starting letters of a name, in this case: C G H.

<summary>This page describes the exterior of the manuscript, specifically the aged leather binding and the blind-stamped initials "C G H" on the front cover.</summary>

The circular emblem is bordered by the text "Hermetic Philosophy" <note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA · HERMETICA"</note>. The pelican is perched on a white cube decorated with four red roses arranged in a square, a symbol often associated with the Rosy Cross. Above the pelican, a golden radiance or sun shines from the top of the oval. The entire design is crowned by a green laurel sprig.</image-desc>

<summary>This is the title page of an alchemical treatise published in 1731, promising clear, non-allegorical instructions for the transmutation of gold and other secret chemical processes. It positions itself as a practical manual for enthusiasts of "noble chemistry," distancing itself from the traditionally cryptic language of earlier alchemists.</summary>

<summary>This title page introduces a 1730 alchemical treatise attributed to an anonymous member of an English order, promising clear and practical instructions for making gold. It distinguishes itself by claiming to avoid the confusing allegories typically found in alchemical texts, offering instead a "clearly manifested" guide for practitioners.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the preface (Vorrede) of the work, following the title page. It establishes the provenance of the chemical recipes, attributing them to a nobleman’s travels and his interactions with European masters of the craft.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring two reclining cherubs (putti) flanking a central oval medallion. Within the medallion, an altar or pedestal supports a heart, which is being illuminated by rays of light emanating from a cloud-shrouded sun or divine eye.</image-desc>

# Preface.

## Kind Reader!

In these few sheets, you will find a collection of various selected <term>processes</term> <note>original: "Processe." These are chemical recipes or experimental procedures.</note> and other <term>art-pieces</term> <note>original: "Kunst-Stücke." In the 18th century, this referred to specialized technical secrets or clever applications of science and craft.</note>, which were obtained by a person of noble rank during travels through Italy, France, and England, at great and heavy expense. This was achieved because the same individual not only visited the greatest and most famous <term>laboratories</term> <note>original: "Laboratoria."</note> but also, through association with <term>virtuosos</term> <note>original: "Virtuosis." At this time, a "virtuoso" was a person of great skill or a dedicated student of the natural sciences.</note> and <term>adepts</term> <note>original: "Adeptis." An adept was someone believed to have achieved the highest mastery of alchemy, specifically the discovery of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, came to possess many secrets.

<meta>catchword: wor-</meta>

<summary>This preface introduces the book's contents as a collection of elite chemical secrets gathered by a nobleman during an expensive "Grand Tour" of Europe's finest laboratories. It establishes the work's authority by claiming the information comes directly from experienced masters and skilled practitioners.</summary>
<keywords>preface, alchemy, chemical processes, Grand Tour, laboratories, Virtuosi, Adepts, 18th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Kind Reader" address from the previous page. It discusses the provenance of the manuscript—translated from the late Count’s multilingual notes—and the editor’s personal verification of some of the alchemical secrets.</meta>

found recorded. Effort has been taken—after receiving from the heirs of the late Count his <term>processes</term> <note>original: "Processe." These are chemical recipes or step-by-step experimental procedures.</note> written in the English, French, Italian, and Latin languages—to translate them into German, and frequently to include the fine reflections he made therein, and even the journal of his conducted work itself. 

I cannot provide anyone with a guarantee that every <term>process</term> which one or another might attempt to <term>elaborate</term> <note>original: "elaboriren." To "elaborate" a process meant to technically execute or work out the chemical preparation in a laboratory.</note> will <term>perform</term> <note>original: "præstiren." This refers to the process "delivering" or achieving the specific result promised in its title.</note> exactly what is stated in the heading. However, I can certainly assure this much: that among them are those which I have worked out myself and found to be true; thus, these few sheets, because of certain very great secrets contained within, are certainly not to be paid for with gold <note>A common idiomatic expression of the time meaning the information is priceless or more valuable than the cost of the paper.</note>. 

On the other hand, lovers of the noble <term>Chemistry</term> <note>original: "Chymie." In this era, chemistry and alchemy were often synonymous, both seeking to understand the hidden properties of matter.</note> will find here and there very advantageous <term>techniques</term> <note>original: "Handgriffe." Literally "hand-grips," these are the practical, manual maneuvers or "tricks of the trade" used by experienced chemists.</note> which one might otherwise seek in other books in vain, so that those—

He highlights that while he cannot guarantee every experiment's success, he has personally verified several "priceless" secrets and unique laboratory techniques.</summary>

### Preface.
<sig>A 3</sig>

[...those] who might wish to work on the **Universal Work**
<note>The "Great Work" (*Magnum Opus*), the ultimate goal of alchemy: the creation of the Philosophers' Stone.</note> of **Count Bernard**,
<note>Likely referring to Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemical author whose works were staples of the Hermetic tradition.</note> will find sufficient **satisfaction**
here regarding the discovery of the **genuine matter**,
<note>The *materia prima* or "first matter"—the fundamental substance needed to begin the alchemical transformation.</note> as well as how the **true seed**
<note>In alchemical theory, minerals and metals were thought to possess a "seed" or life-force that could be extracted and matured.</note> is to be extracted from the **minerals** according to the **Spagyric art**,
<note>The "art of separation and recombination," a term coined by Paracelsus to describe the chemical preparation of medicines and metallic essences.</note> without adding anything foreign to it, with little effort and low cost; and thus, how to obtain the true **Philosophers’ Mercury**,
<note>original: "Mercurius Philosophorum." Not common quicksilver, but a purified, "living" substance that alchemists believed was the essential solvent for the Work.</note> which is sought by so many but found by so few. By this means, everything that **Bernard** has written may thereafter be very easy to understand, and the entire **Work** may be brought to a conclusion with good **success**.

As for the **economic**
<note>original: "Oeconomischen." These refer to practical, industrial, or household recipes (such as making dyes, soaps, or alloys) rather than purely "transmutational" alchemy.</note> matters that are interspersed here, they will recommend themselves sufficiently because of their particular usefulness, and even more so because most of them have been **experimented** upon and found to be **true**. Now, should this collection of such **curious processes**
<note>The term "curious" in the 17th and 18th centuries implied something highly specialized, expert, or revealing hidden secrets of nature.</note> be pleasing to the lovers of **Chemistry**, the **Editor** promises to continue with them, as he still possesses a considerable **collection**,
<note>original: "Apparatum." Here referring to a prepared set of research materials, manuscripts, and laboratory notes.</note> among which...

<summary>The editor explains that the manuscript provides the "genuine matter" and the "Philosophers' Mercury" necessary to understand the works of Count Bernard. He also highlights the inclusion of practical, "economic" chemical recipes and promises further publications if this volume is well-received.</summary>
<keywords>Count Bernard of Trevisan, Universal Work, Philosophers' Mercury, Spagyric art, Economic chemistry, alchemy, first matter, laboratory processes</keywords>

### Preface.

...possesses even more beautiful and curious things to be found. Initially, there was some hesitation to share these **processes**
<term>processes</term>
<note>In this context, "processes" refers to specific chemical or alchemical recipes and procedures for transforming substances.</note> 
with the world, because it is only too well known what a terrible multitude of mostly false processes already lie open to the light of day. These cause many a simpleton—when, out of a desire to become rich, he begins **to labor** 
<term>to labor</term>
<note>original: "laboriren." This refers specifically to performing manual laboratory work or conducting chemical experiments.</note> 
on them—to be deprived of his entire fortune, and thereafter to habitually revile and blaspheme the noble science of **Chemistry**. 
<term>Chemistry</term>
<note>original: "Chymie." During the 17th and 18th centuries, "Chymie" was an umbrella term that included both practical chemical arts (like metallurgy and pharmacy) and the spiritual/transmutational goals of alchemy.</note>
However, because one is certainly assured that a great treasure is hidden among these published works, which has been revealed to those who are worthy, it is hoped that a special service has thereby been rendered to them. In which hope, to the kind reader, for continued goodwill, commends himself

->The AUTHOR.<-
<note>original: "AVTOR."</note>

<meta>catchword: Content.</meta>

<summary>The author concludes the preface by warning against the many fraudulent alchemical "processes" that lead the greedy to ruin, while asserting that this specific collection contains genuine hidden treasures for the worthy reader.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, chemical processes, laboring, laboratory, fraud, chemistry, preface, authorial intent</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the author's preface to the table of contents. The previous page ended with the author's sign-off and a promise of genuine alchemical secrets. This page lists the first four chapters or "processes" found in the book.</meta>

# Contents.
## Of the Materials Con-
## tained in this Work.

->1.<-
A true and proven **separation process**, which yields at least 2 **loth** of gold from a **mark** of silver.
<term>separation process</term>
<note>original: "Scheidungs-Proceſs." A chemical method used to "part" or separate gold from silver, typically using nitric acid or antimony.</note>
<term>mark</term>
<note>original: "Marck." A historical unit of weight for precious metals, roughly equivalent to 234 grams.</note>
<term>loth</term>
<note>original: "Loth." A smaller unit of weight; in the German system, there were 16 loth to one mark.</note>

->2.<-
A wondrous **philosophical tree**, which bears golden and silver fruits.
<term>philosophical tree</term>
<note>original: "philoſophiſcher Baum." Often called "Diana's Tree" (Arbor Dianae), this refers to the crystalline growth of silver or gold from a solution, which alchemists viewed as evidence that metals possessed a form of vegetative life.</note>

->3.<-
A **tincture** made from the **Vitriol of Mars and Venus**, of which one part **tinctures** 1,000 parts of silver into gold that remains stable in all tests. A work of little cost and easy effort.
<term>Vitriol of Mars and Venus</term>
<note>original: "Vitriolo Martis & veneris." In alchemical shorthand, Mars represents iron and Venus represents copper. These are iron sulfate and copper sulfate.</note>
<term>tincture</term>
<note>original: "Tinctur / tingiret." In alchemy, a tincture is a concentrated substance used to "tinge" or transform the inner nature and color of a base metal into a noble one.</note>

->4.<-
A true **particular process** of the **Little Peasant**, by means of which 2 **loth** of gold are to be separated from a **mark** of silver.
<term>particular process</term>
<note>original: "Particular-Proceſs." Unlike the "Universal" work (the search for the Philosopher's Stone), a "particular" process is a specific recipe for transmuting a limited amount of metal or improving its value.</note>
<term>Little Peasant</term>
<note>original: "kleinen Bauers." This likely refers to Johannes Grasshoff, a famous 17th-century alchemist who wrote under the pseudonym "Der kleine Bauer."</note>

<meta>catchword: 5. Silver</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the Table of Contents, listing four specific alchemical recipes (processes) ranging from the separation of precious metals to the creation of a "philosophical tree."</summary>
<keywords>table of contents, separation process, mark of silver, loth of gold, philosophical tree, vitriol of mars, vitriol of venus, tincture, particular process, Johannes Grasshoff</keywords>

# Contents.

---

->5.<-

To make silver and gold so fluid and **tincture-like** that, if one places them upon a glowing copper sheet, they penetrate through and through, **tincturing** the copper into the finest gold.
<term>tincture-like</term>
<note>original: "Tincturalisch." This refers to the property of a "tincture," a concentrated alchemical substance capable of "tingeing" or fundamentally changing the color and quality of a base metal.</note>

->6.<-

A **tincture** such that, when a glowing iron nail is quenched within it, the nail is transformed into gold.

->7.<-

To rid silver of its **cold moisture** in such a way that it is very easily and completely transformed into gold.
<term>cold moisture</term>
<note>original: "kalten Feuchtigkeit." Following ancient Greek theory, alchemists believed silver was dominated by the qualities of coldness and moisture. To turn silver into gold, one had to "ripen" it by removing these excess qualities.</note>

->8.<-

**Dry precipitate**.
<term>precipitate</term>
<note>original: "Niederschlag." A solid substance that emerges from a liquid solution during a chemical reaction.</note>

->9.<-

The true preparation of **carmine**.
<term>carmine</term>
<note>original: "Carmins." A vivid red pigment. In this context, it may refer to a laboratory method for producing the pigment or a related alchemical "redness."</note>

->10.<-

How to make **Oil of Sulfur** in great quantity without the use of a crucible or glass.
<term>Oil of Sulfur</term>
<note>original: "Oleum Sulphuris." An early name for sulfuric acid, typically produced by burning sulfur and collecting the vapors.</note>

->11.<-

A beautiful method for the **Mercury of the Philo-**
<meta>catchword: sopho-</meta>
<term>Mercury of the Philosophers</term>
<note>original: "Mercurium Philosophorum." This is not common mercury (quicksilver), but a specialized, "purified" substance that alchemists believed was the essential root of all metals and the primary ingredient for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

<summary>This page continues the table of contents, listing advanced alchemical procedures for transmuting copper and iron into gold, as well as the production of sulfuric acid and the elusive Mercury of the Philosophers.</summary>

<keywords>transmutation, tincture, copper, silver, gold, cold moisture, carmine, oil of sulfur, Mercury of the Philosophers</keywords>

# Table of Contents.

---

**...double [mercury] of the philosophers**, from which a high **tincture** is afterwards to be prepared.
<term>double mercury of the philosophers</term>
<note>original: "sophorum duplicatum" (abbreviated Latin: philosophorum duplicatum). This refers to a specialized form of mercury "sharpened" or "doubled" with the addition of a metallic soul (often gold or silver) to prepare it for the Great Work.</note>

->12.<-

To extract a <term>salt</term> from the <term>magnet</term>, which is a true <term>balsam of nature</term>; because of its great and wondrous effect, it is not to be paid for with any amount of gold.
<term>salt from the magnet</term>
<note>Alchemists believed the lodestone (magnet) contained a concentrated "attractive" spiritual force. Extracting its "salt" was thought to provide a medicinal balm that could "attract" health and expel disease.</note>

->13.<-

The <term>process</term> of the Count of S., to <term>fix</term> common <term>cinnabar</term> into good silver, which contains much gold.
<term>fix</term>
<note>original: "figiren." Fixation is the process of transforming a volatile or liquid substance (like mercury) into a solid form that can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating.</note>
<term>cinnabar</term>
<note>original: "Zinnober." A bright red mineral (mercury sulfide) and the primary ore of mercury. In alchemy, it was often the starting material for creating silver or gold.</note>

->14.<-

From one **mark** of <term>cinnabar</term>, to make 3 and a half **lots** of silver and one **lot** of gold.
<term>mark / lot</term>
<note>original: "Marck / Loth." These are historical German units of weight. A Mark was roughly 233 grams, and a Lot was approximately 14 to 15 grams.</note>

->15.<-

To make a <term>tincture</term> without fire, vessel, furnace, or other instruments.
<note>This likely refers to a "philosophical" or spiritual process that takes place through natural decomposition (putrefaction) or internal prayer, rather than traditional lab equipment.</note>

->16.<-

<term>Drinkable gold.</term>
<note>original: "Aurum potabile." A legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in such a way that it remains liquid and can be ingested to grant longevity or cure any ailment.</note>

->17.<-

A <term>secret remedy</term> against **dropsy**.
<term>secret remedy</term>
<note>original: "Arcanum." A deep secret or a powerful, hidden medicinal virtue within a substance.</note>
<term>dropsy</term>
<note>original: "Wassersucht." An old medical term for edema, the painful swelling of the body caused by fluid retention.</note>

<meta>catchword: 18. From the</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a list of alchemical recipes and medicinal "arcana," featuring methods for fixing cinnabar into precious metals and preparing "drinkable gold" and magnetic salts.</summary>
<keywords>mercury of the philosophers, salt of the magnet, cinnabar, fixation, aurum potabile, arcanum, dropsy, alchemy units</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the table of contents from the previous page, transitioning from the preparation of mercury to the practical and philosophical extraction of essences from gold and silver ores. ## Table of Contents.
---

->18.<-

On gold ore, its minerals, its effects, its location, and its striking <term>veins</term>.
<term>veins</term>
<note>original: "streichenden Gängen." This is a historical mining term referring to the horizontal direction or "strike" of a mineral deposit as it runs through the earth.</note>

->19.<-

How to extract the **golden seed** from gold ore, and how to make the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> from it.
<term>Philosopher’s Stone</term>
<note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum." The ultimate goal of alchemy; a legendary substance believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and acting as a universal panacea for health.</note>

->20.<-

How to extract the **seed** from silver ore, and thereby to make a **tincture**.
<note>An alchemical "tincture" is a concentrated medicinal or transformative liquid. It was believed that the "seed" or "soul" of a metal could be extracted and used to "tinge" (transform) other substances.</note>

->21.<-

A great secret: how to <term>extract</term> the <term>sulfur</term> from gold by means of a **sweet spirit of salt**.
<term>extract</term>
<note>original: "extrahiren."</note>
<term>sulfur</term>
<note>original: "Schwefel." In the alchemical "Tria Prima" (Three Primes), Sulfur represented the soul and the principle of combustibility and color within a metal, rather than just the chemical element sulfur.</note>
<note>original: "Spiritum salis dulcem." A "sweet spirit of salt" refers to a less corrosive or "sweetened" form of hydrochloric acid, often distilled with alcohol (producing what we now know as ethyl chloride), used as a powerful solvent.</note>

->22.<-

An **extraction-water**, which His <term>Serene Highness</term> of M... received from a **philosopher**, by means of which he <term>extracts</term> the <term>sulfur</term> from gold, and with it, in an easy manner, transforms silver into gold.
<term>Serene Highness</term>
<note>original: "Hochfürstl. Durchl." (Hochfürstliche Durchlaucht). A title used for high-ranking royalty or reigning princes.</note>
<term>philosopher</term>
<note>original: "Philosopho." In early modern texts, a "philosopher" often specifically referred to an alchemist or someone who understood the secret laws of nature.</note>

<summary>This page lists entries 18 through 22 of the table of contents, focusing on the extraction of "seeds" and "sulfur" from gold and silver to create the Philosopher's Stone and facilitate transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>gold ore, silver ore, golden seed, Philosopher's Stone, alchemical tincture, sulfur of gold, spirit of salt, transmutation, metallurgy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the table of contents from the previous page, which concluded with an entry about an extraction water for minerals. The numbering continues from 23 to 30, shifting from metallurgical transformations to medicinal panaceas and industrial inventions.</meta>

# Contents.
---

->23.<-
->How to make iron as soft as lead,<-

->24.<-
->A beautiful <term>graduation</term> for gold.<-
<term>graduation</term>
<note>original: "Gradirung." In alchemy and metallurgy, graduation refers to a process used to purify or increase the "grade" (purity and color) of a precious metal.</note>

->25.<-
->How to turn gold into a <term>tincture</term> through <term>cementing</term>.<-
<term>tincture</term>
<note>original: "Tinctur." Here, it refers to a potent alchemical substance capable of transmitting its properties—such as color or medicinal power—to other materials.</note>
<term>cementing</term>
<note>original: "cementiren." This is a process of refining metals by heating them in a "cement"—a dry powder made of salts and other minerals—which draws out impurities.</note>

->26.<-

# I.

### A true and tested
### parting process, which yields
### at least 2 Loth of gold
### from one Mark of silver.
<note>In historical German weight systems, a "Mark" was roughly 233 grams. A "Loth" was approximately 14 to 17 grams. This recipe claims to extract a significant portion of gold—nearly 12%—from silver ore or alloy.</note>

Take <note>original: "Rec." an abbreviation for the Latin "Recipe," the traditional opening for medical and chemical instructions.</note> saltpeter <note>original: "Nitrum." Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in making acids.</note> and alum, 2 pounds of each. First, let the alum melt in a glazed pot over a gentle charcoal fire. When it has melted, add the saltpeter shortly thereafter and stir it well together so that the saltpeter also melts therein. Let it stand over the charcoal fire, stirring frequently, until it begins to harden. Then remove it from the heat and let the material cool in the pot. Take it out and crush it coarsely, to the size of a hazelnut or bean. Mix in two or three times its weight of dry potter’s clay. Place it into a Waldenburg retort <note>Waldenburg, in Saxony, was famous for its high-quality stoneware that could withstand the intense heat of chemical distillation.</note> and distill it with an open fire into *aquafortis* <note>original: "Aquafort." Literally "strong water," this is nitric acid, used by goldsmiths to "part" or separate gold from silver, as it dissolves silver but leaves gold intact.</note>, as is customary. This *aquafortis* you shall afterwards purify and separate from its dregs <note>original: "fecibus," from the Latin "faeces," meaning dregs, sediment, or impurities.</note> as follows: Take one dram <note>original: "Quentlein," a small unit of weight, roughly 3.6 grams.</note> of fine silver, beaten into thin laminates <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "niret" suggests the word "lamini-ret" (laminated/beaten thin).</note>

<summary>This page introduces the first practical recipe of the manuscript: a method for creating nitric acid (aquafortis) to separate gold from silver using saltpeter, alum, and a specialized clay-lined distillation process.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, gold refining, parting process, nitric acid, saltpeter, alum, Waldenburg retort, distillation</keywords>

### *Note on the Reduction of the Calx.*

Regarding the reduction [restoration to metallic form] of the <term>Solar Silver Calx</term> <note>original: "Solarischen Silber-Kalcks." In alchemy, "Solar" refers to Gold. Since, even if some <term>spirit of aquafort</term> <note>Nitric acid residues or fumes.</note> remains with it after the annealing, this can do no harm at all—because soon another <term>Aquafort</term>, prepared similarly in several respects, will be added to it—but is rather useful and beneficial to the work.

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Separation Process, etc.</header>

If one refines lead with the mentioned addition of other silver, then nothing special will be lost from the <term>Silver</term> <note>original: "Luna," the alchemical name for silver, associated with the moon.</note>. But it is much better if one gradually introduces the washed-out calx <note>The fine powder resulting from the chemical process.</note> into three times as much molten lead in a crucible, and then refines them together carefully on the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Capellen," a shallow, porous dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead.</note> without adding more lead. Or <term>Note Well</term> <note>original: "NB."</note>, which gives the very best <term>reduction</term> <note>The process of restoring a metal from its powdered or oxidized state back to its metallic form.</note>: one mixes three parts of granulated lead and one part of the previously calcined and washed-out calx together, and lets it melt first very gently, and afterwards thoroughly before the bellows, and then refines it without more lead.

If, however, one were to melt and refine the calx unwashed and only calcined <term>by itself</term> <note>original: "per se"</note> according to the process, the <term>spirits</term> of the <term>nitric acid</term> <note>original: "Aquaforts," referring to the acidic vapors or residues still trapped in the powder.</note> which are still within it would rob almost most of the silver along with the gold, and then turn the remainder entirely into slag. Therefore, it is best that one carries the same—well-washed as mentioned just before—into the lead within a <term>melting crucible</term>, and only then refines it on the <term>test</term> <note>original: "Teſt," another term for the cupel or refining hearth.</note>. This is for the reason that the lead, through its "sweetness," softens the sharp <term>spirits of nitric acid</term> <note>original: "Spiritus Aquafortis"</note> and, through its "watery coldness," cools the <term>calx</term> <note>original: "Calcem"</note>—which has become very <term>irritable</term> <note>original: "heißgrätig," a metallurgical term for metal that is difficult to melt or behaves stubbornly due to impurities; literally "hot-boned."</note> through the sharp waters—and brings it out of its slag-like nature into a metallic form and shape.

### How to Improve This Process.

Take at the beginning, for the <term>ferment</term> <note>A substance added to a mixture to trigger a chemical or alchemical transformation.</note> of the first <term>mercurial nitric acid</term> <note>original: "Mercurial Aquafortes"; a potent acidic solvent containing mercury.</note>, 2 or perhaps 3 or 4 <term>ducat-gold</term> <note>High-purity gold from minted coins, used here as a raw material.</note> (for the more, the better); **dissolve** these—hammered into very thin sheets and rolled up—in the special <term>nitric acid</term> prepared with <term>butter of antimony</term> <note>original: "Butyro Antimonii"; antimony trichloride, a corrosive chemical used in refining.</note> and mercury; and place this **solution** into a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: "Balneum," a gentle, consistent heat source.</note> for 3 days to **digest** <note>original: "digeriren"; the process of slow heating to encourage chemical breakdown or maturation.</note> for the sake of better "opening up" the gold; then draw the acid off again; thus the gold remains lying dry at the bottom; **dissolve** this a second and third time, or more often (for the more frequent, the better it is), with fresh, specially prepared acid; each time let it <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: "putrificiren"; in this context, it refers to a slow chemical decomposition or maturation under heat rather than organic rotting.</note> for 3 days and draw it dry every time; the last time, however, draw it off so that the gold remains behind with the thickness of honey; then pour this into the other ordinary <term>mercurial nitric acid</term> as a <term>ferment</term>. 

In the same way, one can also proceed with the latter <term>ferment</term> of the <term>green water</term> <note>original: "grünen Wasses"; likely an acidic solution containing copper or iron salts, giving it a green tint.</note>; then it will yield a surplus of 4, 5, or 6 <term>loth</term> <note>A unit of weight, roughly 15-17 grams.</note> of good gold per <term>mark</term> <note>A unit of weight for precious metals, roughly 230-250 grams.</note> of silver; ***Note Well:*** because in the first <term>fermented</term> mercurial water, during the dissolution of the silver, a full 7 <term>loth</term> of **calx** <note>The powdery metallic residue left after a metal has been dissolved or burned.</note>—which does not withstand the <term>quartation</term> <note>original: "Quart"; a standard assaying method where gold is purified by alloying it with silver and then dissolving the silver away.</note> and other gold tests, except for the <term>antimony</term>—will fall, and it still lacks the gold color; which **calx**, however, when

...position and discharge, began to become reddish, until finally everything looked like **Ruby**; then I strengthened the fire and continued with it for 6 hours; finally, for the last 3 hours, I gave strong fire at the bottom and top until the glasses became white again.

**T**his day I <term>sealed</term> <note>original: "verlutiret"; from "luting," the practice of sealing joints in alchemical apparatus with a clay-like paste to prevent vapors from escaping.</note> the <term>crucibles</term> <note>original: "Tiegel"; heat-resistant ceramic containers used for high-temperature chemical reactions.</note> with the <term>cement</term> <note>In this context, "cement" refers to a specific alchemical mixture used for refining or sealing, rather than modern construction cement.</note> so they could dry nicely and gradually. The **lute** <note>original: "Lutum"; the sealing paste itself.</note> was glue mixed with salt.

**O**n the 3rd of April, I again <term>distilled</term> <note>original: "getrieben" (driven); the process of using heat to drive a substance out of a mixture and into a receiver.</note> the **Butter of Antimony** <note>original: "Butyrum Antimonii"; antimony trichloride, an oily, caustic substance that was highly valued in alchemical medicine and metallurgy.</note> and used the <term>retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck.</note> with the third portion as the <term>receiver</term> <note>original: "Vorlage"; the vessel that catches the distilled liquid.</note>; the time and the fire were the same as the first time; it did not yet turn red, but only yellowish.

**O**n the same day <note>original: "Eod." for the Latin "Eodem die."</note>, the **strong water** <note>original: "Aquafort" (Aqua Fortis); nitric acid.</note> was <term>precipitated</term> <note>original: "gefället"; to cause a solid to separate from a solution.</note> with half a <term>dram</term> <note>original: "Quentchen"; a small unit of weight, approximately 3.7 grams.</note> of silver; thus the <term>sediment</term> <note>original: "Feces"; literally "dregs," referring here to the solid precipitate.</note> settled to the bottom as white as snow.

**O**n the 4th of April, I prepared both cements as the process requires: namely, 2 hours gentle [heat], 2 hours stronger, and 2 hours completely covered with coals.

<margin>Note well.</margin> I placed both crucibles next to each other, yet so that there was a handbreadth of **space** <note>original: "Spatium"</note> between them.

**O**n the 5th of April, I [processed] the last portion of **Antimony** <note>original: "Antimonii"</note>...

...of **antimony** <note>original: "Antimonii"</note> and **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurii"</note> sublimated, let them stand in <term>digestion</term> <note>original: "Digestion"; a process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to encourage chemical change.</note> with the **Butter** <note>original: "Butyro"; referring to the "Butter of Antimony" mentioned on the previous page.</note>; and the subtle **Sulfur of Antimony** <note>original: "Sulphur Antimonii"; in alchemy, "sulfur" often referred to the combustible or "soul" principle of a mineral, associated here with the red color.</note> sublimated beautifully red in the neck of the <term>retort</term>, which was a sign that the **Butter** would become red.
**NB.** <note>Latin abbreviation for "Nota Bene," meaning "Note well."</note> The **digestion** must be quite gentle, and specifically such that only the belly <note>The rounded main body of the glass vessel.</note> of the glass becomes warm, but not the neck; otherwise the best volatile particles will escape. It must also be well-stopped with a damp linen cloth.

On the 6th of April, I <term>distilled</term> <note>original: "getrieben" (driven); the application of heat to drive vapors into a receiver.</note> the **Butter** for the third time, and then it became quite red and no longer settled so heavily in the neck. However, I had to distill it for 7 hours and eventually give such a strong fire that the **retort** completely collapsed. <note>original: "zusammen suncke"; the glass became so hot that it softened and slumped or melted.</note>

With each distillation, I obtained some **Cinnabar of Antimony**, <note>original: "Zinabr. Antimonii"; a deep red mineral (mercury sulfide) that forms as a byproduct in this specific reaction.</note> though more the last time than the first two, which had sublimated in the neck of the retort.

On this day, I also distilled the <term>Graduation Water</term> <note>original: "Gradir-Wasser"; a powerful acidic solution, often containing nitric acid and salts, used to "graduate" (purify or intensify the color/quality) of metals, especially gold.</note> over the course of 6 hours—first with gentle heat and finally with strong fire, though no heat was applied from above; the <term>residue</term> <note>original: "Caput Mort." (Caput Mortuum); literally "dead head," the useless solid remains left in the vessel after distillation.</note> was entirely red, like a liver.

...there it became a beautiful dark ruby red; in total I obtained 6 <term>Loth</term> <note>A German unit of weight, roughly 14–16 grams or half an ounce.</note>, well weighed. The work lasted a little more than 5 hours; when the mists appeared, I removed the fire from the furnace.

On the 9th of April, I mixed the <term>Aqua Fortis</term> <note>original: "Aquafort"; nitric acid, a powerful solvent.</note> or **Spirit of Nitre** <note>original: "Spiritum Nitri"; another name for nitric acid.</note> with 2 and 1/4 <term>Loth</term> of **Butter** <note>original: "Butyro"; referring to Butter of Antimony (antimony trichloride).</note> and distilled it over the <term>helm</term> <note>original: "Helm"; the "head" or cap of a distillation apparatus that directs vapors into a cooling vessel.</note>; I spent 6 hours on this and obtained a white **residue** <note>original: "Residuum".</note>, which is called <term>Bezoarticum minerale</term> <note>Mineral Bezoar; a white powder (typically antimony pentoxide) used in historical medicine as a sweat-inducing agent and counter-poison.</note>, and in which the 1/2 <term>Quenten</term> <note>A small unit of weight, about 3.6 grams or 1/4 of a Loth.</note> of **silver**, with which the **Aqua Fortis** had been precipitated, must be present.

During the <term>driving</term> <note>original: "Treiben"; the process of distillation using heat.</note> I had to proceed very cautiously because the <term>flask</term> <note>original: "Kolben".</note>, **helm**, and **receivers** <note>original: "Vorlagen"; the vessels that catch the condensed vapors.</note> were not especially large; otherwise, everything would have shattered, for the glasses became so dark red that one could not see through them.

**NB.** <note>Abbreviation for the Latin "Nota Bene," meaning "Note well."</note> When I poured the **Butter** into the **Aqua Fortis**, it became like milk and remained so in the flask until it became dry; but what passed over was a **red spirit**.

# Separation Process, etc.

On the 15th, the second part of the **nitric acid** <note>original: "Aquafort"; a powerful corrosive acid.</note> and the **royal water** <note>original: "Aqua Regis"; a mixture of acids named "royal" for its unique ability to dissolve the "noble" metal gold.</note> was prepared: namely, 1 pound of common **nitric acid** and 14 **Loth** <note>A German unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of molten **common salt** <note>original: "Sal commune"; sodium chloride.</note>, which yielded 11 **Loth**.

On the evening of the 16th, it was distilled from **gold**, and on Wednesday morning I found that the **gold** had sublimated <note>original: "sublimiret"; the process where a solid turns into vapor and then solidifies again, often as a crust on the glass.</note> around the belly of the **small flask** <note>original: "Kölblein".</note> to a width of three knife-backs <note>A measurement referring to the thickness of the spine of a knife blade.</note> all the way around. This is a sign that the **solvent** <note>original: "Menstrum"; a liquid used to dissolve a solid.</note> had dissolved more than just the physical body. What had passed over in the distillation looked beautifully yellowish.

Early on the 17th, I again poured a portion of fresh **spirit** <note>original: "Spiritum"; a distilled liquid.</note> over it and set it to **digestion** <note>A process of gentle, continuous heating used to encourage chemical reactions.</note>. The **gold** was only half dissolved.

The day before, on the 16th, I poured the **royal water** onto the **cement** <note>In alchemy, "cement" often refers to a powdered mixture used to refine gold or the residue resulting from such a process.</note>, and early on the 17th, I set the other half-**Loth** minus 7 **grains** <note>original: "Gran"; a very small unit of weight, roughly 0.06 grams.</note> of **ducat gold** <note>High-purity gold used for minting coins.</note> into **digestion** with **royal water**.

Early on the 18th, the **royal water** on the **gold** already appeared red and had mostly dissolved it.

In the evening, the first [substance] was distilled for the second time.

On this day, I combined the 11 **Loth** of **spirit of nitre** <note>original: "Spirit. Nitri"; another term for nitric acid.</note> with 2 **Loth** minus 1 **Quentchen** <note>original: "Quenten"; a small weight, about 3.6 grams.</note> of **butter** <note>original: "Butyro"; referring to Butter of Antimony.</note> and distilled it over the **helm** <note>The "head" or cap of the distillation apparatus that directs vapors into a cooling vessel.</note> just like the first time; I obtained a similar white **residue** <note>original: "Resid."</note>. I distilled it for 8 hours and was left with a net amount of 2 **Loth** of **butter of antimony** <note>original: "Butyr. Antimonii".</note>.

<header>VIII. Dry Precipitation</header>

apply a gentle fire at first / so that the **sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur"; in this context, it refers to the reactive chemical element used to separate or bond with metals</note> creeps through the **silver** (NB. 2 **loth** <term>loth<gloss>a historical German unit of weight, approximately 14.6 grams or half an ounce</gloss></term> of **copper** must have been melted into the silver) when it then flows / let 8 **loth** of **lead** melt and pour it around the top of the **crucible** / throw in 2 **loth** of **melted salt** / covered with a brick and let it flow for three-quarters of an hour / then poured out and the <term>plagma<gloss>original: Plagma; # X. How to make Oil of Sulphur

...what remains on the bottom and the sides is **carmine**; let that dry by itself.

->10.<-

### How to make Oil of Sulphur in large quantities, without a crucible or glass

First, melt as much **sulphur** <note>original: *Sulphur*; sulfur was a foundational substance in early chemistry and alchemy</note> as you believe you will need, and soak it up with small pieces of linen cloth so that all the sulphur sticks to them. When this is ready, take a vessel of convenient size and fill it to the fourth or sixth part with water. Then light a small piece of the sulfur-cloth and place it into the <term>bung hole</term> <note>the opening in a barrel or cask through which liquid is poured</note>, sealing it well with the <term>bung</term> <note>a stopper or plug used to seal a cask</note>; let it burn inside. When you believe it has finished burning, shake the little barrel back and forth so that the sulphur <note>referring here to the sulfurous fumes or "spirit" produced by combustion</note> may also combine with the water. After this, light one piece after another, shaking the vessel well each time, until all the sulphur has burned and the water has attained a pleasant *acidity*, from which the water can then be drawn off <note>this describes the production of dilute sulfuric acid; "drawing off" likely refers to concentrated the acid by evaporating the water</note>.

XI. Method for making Mercury

### II.

## A beautiful method to make the **doubled Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Mercurium Philosophorum duplicatum*; a potent alchemical version of mercury believed to be essential for the Great Work</note> / from which a high **Tincture** <note>original: *Tinctur*; a powerful substance capable of transmuting metals or creating a medicinal elixir</note> is to be prepared afterwards.

Take 2 parts **Sublimated Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurii Sublimati*; mercuric chloride</note> and one part **Orpiment** <note>original: *Auripigment*; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, grind each separately quite fine in a glass mortar / mix them well together and put them into a glass **retort** <note>original: *Retorte*; a flask with a long, downward-pointing neck</note> of which the third part must remain empty / seal a receiver (it is not necessary that it be large) / and distill in sand **by degrees** <note>original: *per gradus*; gradually increasing the temperature</note>, until nothing more passes over / then give very strong fire from below and also place coals around the top / thus the **cinnabar** <note>original: *Cinober*; mercury sulfide</note> sublimes and one gets nearly as much of it as there was Sublimated Mercury.

This cinnabar, when **filed silver** <note>original: *Lunam Limatam*; Luna is the alchemical name for silver</note> is layered with it / according to the common process / is said to yield much gold in the silver.

The **liquid** <note>original: *Liquor*</note> that has come over is fuming / poisonous, stinking / and very corrosive; this is separated using a **separating glass** <note>original: *vitrum separatorium*; a vessel used to separate liquids of different densities</note> from a yellowish oil / which floats on top / which is thrown away.

Now distill the liquid **by itself** <note>original: *per se*</note> from glass retorts, until there is no more dirty...

XI. Method for the Mercury of the Philosophers 49

...leaves no more spots in the **retort** <note>original: *Retorte*; a glass vessel with a long neck used for distillation</note>, it is sufficiently purified; it requires several distinct **distillations** <note>original: *destillationes*</note>, but each can be completed in 3 to 4 hours.

One drops this **liquor** <note>original: *Liquorem*</note> into a large **sugar jar** <note>original: *Zucker Glaß*; a tall, wide-mouthed glass vessel</note> nearly filled with distilled rain water, so that it falls to the bottom like a **Mercury of Life** <note>original: *Mercurius vitæ*; historically, this was a white precipitate of antimony, but here refers to the alchemical precipitate formed by this specific process</note>. When everything has settled, one pours the water away and **washes** <term>edulcorate: the process of washing a precipitate with water to remove acids or salts</term> the **simple Mercury of Life or of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Mercurium vitæ sive Philosoph. Simplicem*</note> in the very best way with hot distilled rain water until all **corrosives** <note>original: *corrosiva*; the acidic or burning substances</note> are removed; subsequently, one dries it gently.

One puts this **Simple Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium Simplicem*</note> into fine silver that has been beaten as thin as paper, folded into corners so that only a little of this mercury is placed into each silver leaf. Place these together into a **matrass** <note>original: *Kolben*; a long-necked glass flask</note>, put on an **alembic head** <note>original: *Helm*</note>, and apply **subliming fire** <note>original: *sublimir Feuer*; high heat intended to turn a solid into vapor</note>; thus the mercury **sublimes** <note>the process where a substance turns from solid to vapor and back to solid</note> through these silver leaves and leaves all its combustible **sulfur** <note>original: *Schwefel*</note> and **earthiness** <note>original: *terrestritæt*</note> behind.

One rubs 10 parts of this thus-purified **Simple Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium Simplic.*</note> well together with 2 parts of **calx of gold** <note>original: *Gold-Kalck*; gold that has been chemically reduced to a fine powder</note>, and puts it into a small flask with a head upon it; **digest**...

->to make the doubled [mercury]<-

...**digests** <note>The original OCR "riret" is likely the conclusion of the German *digiriret*, meaning to heat a substance gently over a long period.</note> it for several hours; consequently, one **sublimates** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify and concentrate the substance</term> it.

One grinds the **sublimate** <note>original: *sublimirte*; the solid substance that has reformed after being vaporized</note> again with the **residue** <note>original: *Remanenz*; the material left behind at the bottom of the vessel</note> and sublimates it once more; in the 7th or 8th **sublimation**, it will already begin to sublimate with a yellowish color. One repeats the sublimations until it sublimates a complete **ruby red** <note>original: *Rubin roth*</note>, which is a sign that the **Mercury** has saturated itself with the **Sulphur of Gold** <term>Sulphur of Gold (Sulphure Solis): in alchemical theory, this is the "soul" or the active, coloring principle extracted from gold</term>. This is the **Doubled Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Mercurius Philosophor. duplicatus*</note>.

Of this **Doubled Mercury**, one takes 3, 4, or even 5 parts and one part **gold calx** <term>gold calx (Gold-Kalck): gold that has been reduced to a fine, chemically altered powder, often through heating or acid</term>, grinds them well together, and puts the mixture into a **vial** <note>original: *Phiole*</note> so that only a third of the **belly** <note>original: *Bauchs*; the wide, rounded part of the glass flask</note> is filled. When it has been **sealed shut** <note>original: *zugeschmelzet*; literally "melted shut," meaning the glass neck was heated and twisted to create an airtight seal</note>, lay the vial on its side and apply heat so that it sublimates gently. Consequently, one turns the vial over so that the **sublimated matter** mixes again with that which remained at the bottom; and one continues this for as long as it takes until it remains **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so that it no longer vaporizes or "flies" from the fire</term> at the bottom.

# XII. A Salt from [the Magnet]

Decant <note>original: *cantire*; likely a shorthand or variant for *decantieren*, to pour off liquid without disturbing the sediment</note> it until clean; filter it, and let the water evaporate from it until a juice remains that is like an oil. Pour distilled water over this once more and extract the tincture again, so that more dregs <term>feces: the solid sediment or impurities remaining after a liquid has been filtered or poured off</term> will remain behind. Then bring it to an oil or powder; that is the **essence** <term>essence: the concentrated internal virtue or purest part of a substance</term>.

### Salt of the Magnet. <note>original: *Sal Magnetis*</note>

Take the very first **dregs**, together with the others from the **extraction** of the water; let them be **calcined** <term>calcine: to heat a substance to high temperatures in a crucible to drive off volatile components and reduce it to a powder</term> most strongly in a brick kiln; then pour the vinegar over it to extract it. Afterwards, filter and **coagulate** <term>coagulate: to thicken or solidify a liquid into a solid mass through evaporation or cooling</term> it, so that a gray salt remains behind. After 4 days of **digestion** <term>digestion: a process of applying gentle, consistent heat to a substance over a long period, often in a sealed vessel</term> with distilled rainwater, filter it again and evaporate it, so that a white salt remains. **Calcine** this in a crucible—though not too strongly—and extract it again with distilled rainwater. This must be repeated 16 times. In this way, one will obtain a sweet, lovely salt, red and shining like a ruby. This salt is the **Balsam of Nature** <note>In alchemy, this refers to a life-preserving substance or universal healing agent inherent in matter</note>; and if this is used for the **composition** <note>original: *Composition*; the final alchemical mixture or Great Work</note>, one will experience great wonders as to what it will do. With this salt, one can heal all wounds in 24 hours without any danger of rot <note>original: *Fäulung*; decay or gangrene</note>; it also cleanses all corrupted injuries—

# XIII. The Count of S.’s Process, etc.

pour more **Spirit of Vitriol** <term>Spirit of Vitriol: a concentrated sulfuric acid produced from vitriol (metallic sulfates)</term> upon it, and draw it off; once it has **fixed** <note>fixed: in alchemy, to "fix" a substance means to make a volatile material solid and resistant to heat so that it does not evaporate</note> itself, use it for the following **process** <note>process: an alchemical recipe or procedure</note>.

**Take** <term>Rec.: shorthand for the Latin *Recipe*, meaning "Take"</term> 6 **Loth** <note>Loth: a traditional German unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 14–17 grams or half an ounce</note> of Silver and 4 **Loth** of Lead; let them melt together and cool down again slightly, until, according to the well-known test, a straw dipped into it does not burn, but only scorches black; then pour in 4 **Loth** of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver (original: *Mercurium vivum*): liquid mercury, a primary ingredient in the search for the Philosopher's Stone</term>, which has been heated beforehand; stir it until it becomes cold. 

Wash it afterwards with vinegar and salt until no more blackness remains; let it dry and place it in a **cementing box** <term>cementing box: a specialized crucible or container used for heating materials in layers to induce a chemical change</term>. With this and the **cinnabar** <note>cinnabar: a bright red mineral composed of mercury sulfide</note>, create **layer upon layer** <term>stratum super stratum: a common alchemical technique of alternating layers of different substances within a vessel</term> (or mix the cinnabar with egg white, make a dough from it and form little cakes in the box; then place the **amalgam** <term>amalgam: a mixture or alloy of mercury with another metal, in this case, silver and lead</term> upon them, and **continue** <note>original: *continuiret*</note> alternately in this way until the box is nearly full). 

**Seal it well with lute** <term>verlutire: to seal a vessel airtight using a paste made of clay, lime, or other materials to prevent the escape of vapors</term> and place it into a larger iron box; let it warm thoroughly, and keep adding lead alongside the inner box so that it melts and eventually covers the inner box. In this way, keep the **lead** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for the metal lead; original: *Saturnum*</term> in the fire for 8 days without ceasing in a **reverberatory furnace** <term>reverberatory furnace: a furnace designed to reflect heat from the roof onto the material being heated</term>, or as you see fit; then take the box out and **refine the matter on a cupel** <term>treib... auf der Capellen ab: the process of cupellation, where base metals like lead are oxidized and absorbed into a porous dish, leaving refined silver or gold behind</term>; in this way, you shall carry away a magnificent profit.

<summary>This page details a specific alchemical procedure involving the creation of a silver-lead-mercury amalgam and its treatment with cinnabar and "Saturn" (lead) in a furnace. The process concludes with cupellation to extract precious metals, promising the practitioner a significant profit.</summary>

## Concerning Gold Ore, its Rocks, Action, Location, and Stretching Veins.

Gold is formed in its own rocky veins from the most beautiful mother, the purest and most constant of earths, as **Basilius Valentinus** <note>a legendary, likely pseudonymous, 15th-century alchemist and monk credited with influential texts</note> says in his **Last Testament**, page 2, chapter 3. It arises from the most perfect **salt**, **sulfur**, and **mercury** <note>original: *Salz, Schweffel und Mercurio*; these are the 'Tria Prima,' the three primary substances alchemists believed made up all matter</note>, purified of all its dregs <note>original: *fecibus*</note>, and in pure spirits, with the addition of the natural, highly purified heaven—white, yellow, and red sulfur-earths—according to the fiery nature of the Sun. It is so highly constant that there is nothing among all metals that is of a higher, more solid, or heavier body, made from its golden matter, in which no foulness <note>original: *felligkeit*</note> remains...

It is also formed in a sparry limestone and is granular, interspersed with blackish little "eyes" or tiny quartzes, veined, and dripped. In the most subtle solid rocks, it is also found in a striped shale-work, in beautiful clear veins that are mixed with a blue hornstone <term>Hornstein</term> <note>a tough, flint-like variety of quartz</note> and shale; also in some parts as flat quartz seams <term>Quartzfletze</term>, wherein gold is formed as a coating in all the fissures. It is mixed with green gritty material and iron marks, sometimes also in a leading iron-trace or perforated quartz druses <term>Quarz-Drusen</term> <note>cavities lined with crystals</note>, yet most often in gravelly debris. Often, at times, it is found native in brown-black quartz veins...

### To Extract the Golden Seed from Gold Ore, and How to Make the Philosopher's Stone Therefrom.

There is surely no one who would deny that flowers, herbs, and trees are brought forth through their seeds created by God, and that animals are generated in the same manner; why then should one doubt that the same occurs in the mineral kingdom <term>Regno minerali</term>? If, therefore, all metals stem and spring from one root or seminal principle <term>Principio seminali</term> and differ only according to their degree of maturity <term>maturität</term> and fixity <term>fixität</term> <note>the chemical property of a substance that allows it to remain stable and non-volatile when exposed to intense heat</note>, then it is easy to perceive that they can consequently be worked even better, made riper and more stable through art which imitates nature. Thus, the imperfect may be transmuted <note>transformed from one element into another</note> into the most perfect. In this, however, an artist <note>the alchemist or "art-worker"</note> must conduct himself no differently than a wise and experienced gardener or farmer; namely, if he expects fruit, he must [the...]

## XIX. Concerning the Golden Seed, etc.

...found more closely, more perfectly, and more easily than in the gold ore itself. Therefore, take the very purest gold ore that you can find—as *Basilius Valentinus* <note>original: *Basilius Valentinus*; a legendary 15th-century alchemist, often cited as an authority on the properties of antimony and gold</note> also teaches—which carries no other metal with it. From this, make and prepare a beautiful and pure <term>schlich</term> <note>original: *Schlich*; a fine, washed ore concentrate or slime created after crushing and cleaning the raw ore</note>; dry it in the gentle warmth of the sun or in dry air, without using a natural fire. 

Afterwards, grind it very finely on a stone or marble; place it in a glass with a flat bottom, one and a half spans high. Seal it shut at the top or seal it <term>hermetically</term> <note>original: *Hermeticé*; an airtight seal created by melting the glass neck shut, named after the mythical founder of alchemy, Hermes Trismegistus</note>. Place it into a small oak barrel; cover it with a lid so that the head of the glass extends out above the lid (for this reason, the lid should be made of two pieces joined together). Place the little barrel into a kettle and fill the kettle with water. This kettle should be built into a furnace like a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: *Balneum*; specifically a *balneum Mariae*, a method of indirect heating that ensures the temperature never exceeds the boiling point of water</note>. Give it a gentle fire with coals, so that the water does not boil, but is warm enough that one can always bear to keep a hand in it. Thus, the seed of the gold rises upward into the head [of the glass], beautiful and transparent; a considerable portion of it <term>coagulates</term> <note>to thicken or solidify from a vaporous or liquid state</note>, which is called the <term>male seed</term> <note>original: *Mannes Saamen*; in alchemical theory, the "male" principle (often associated with sulfur) provides the "form" or "soul" to the metal, while the "female" (mercury) provides the "body"</note>. 

That this is now the right <term>First Matter</term> <note>original: *erſte Materie*; the primordial, undifferentiated substance from which all metals are believed to grow</note> of the metals, I could prove from many <term>sayings of the philosophers</term> <note>original: *dictis Philosophorum*; references to the traditional authorities of alchemical literature</note>—

<summary>This page provides practical instructions for extracting the "Seed of Gold" from pure ore, describing a process of gentle heating in a hermetically sealed vessel within a water bath. The resulting transparent, solidified substance is identified as the "male seed," the essential First Matter for the alchemical work.</summary>

64 XIX. Concerning the Golden Seed, etc.

...if space would allow; at least I will mention what the well-known *Bernhardus* <note>original: *Bernhardus*; likely Bernhardus Trevisanus, a celebrated 15th-century alchemist who wrote on the "Natural Philosophy of Metals"</note> says about it, when he says: our work is made from one root and two <term>mercurial substances</term> <note>original: *Mercurialischen substantzien*; the fluid, volatile principles believed to be the "spirit" or "soul" of matter</note>, and purely drawn from the <term>minerals</term> <note>original: *Minern*; raw ores as they are found in the earth, rather than refined metals</note>. However, he remained silent regarding the <term>method</term> <note>original: *Modum*; the specific procedure or "way" of operating</note> by which such a seed is to be drawn from the minerals. Furthermore, he requires that the male and female seeds be combined in the proper proportion. Therefore, it is necessary that I also teach how the female seed is to be obtained.

### Extraction of the Female Seed.

Take the very best mercury ore, crush it finely, and grind it just like the aforementioned gold ore. Place it also into such a glass vessel and extract its seed in the manner previously described. This seed is entirely white, like a crystal; it clings like pearls to the head <note>The "head" or "cap" of the distillation vessel where the vapors condense and solidify</note> and <term>coagulates</term> <note>original: *coaguliret*; the process of a liquid or vapor thickening into a solid or semi-solid mass</note>. When the glass is broken, one must scrape it out and preserve it in a glass jar away from the air, so that it does not <term>dissolve</term> <note>original: *solviret*; to turn into a liquid state, often by absorbing moisture from the atmosphere (deliquescence)</note> and run away into water or oil. It is powerfully corrosive; if it touches the hands, it eats away at the skin and fouls them. It is called the Female Seed.

### Composition of the Work.

# XIX. Concerning the Golden Seed, etc.

Now that one has enough of both seeds, one must also know how to mix them together in the proper weight. The **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*; referring here to alchemists, who were often called "Natural Philosophers"</note> have locked this away with a strong bolt <note>This indicates that the exact proportions were considered a great secret of the Great Work</note>. Some say one should take 1 part to 2 parts. **Bernhardus** <note>Bernhardus Trevisanus</note> says, "I pinch the fountains" <note>original: *ich pfetze die fontinen*; an allegorical reference to controlling the flow of the mercurial "waters" in Trevisanus's famous parable of the King's Fountain</note>. **Basilius Valentinus** says one should take the weight as it benefits the matter. **Alanus** <note>Alanus ab Insulis, a medieval scholar and alchemist</note> says that in the combination of both seeds, one should add 9 parts of the **purified Mercury** and one part of the **seed of the Gold Ore**. We shall also remain with this very same proportion.

Therefore, take 9 parts of the **Mercury Ore seed** and 1 part of the **Male Seed** <note>the gold seed</note>; rub them well together in a glass dish, and put them into a **phial** <note>a small glass flask</note> that holds ten times as much as has been put in. Treat it with **lamp-fire** <note>original: *Lampen-Feuer*; a steady, gentle heat provided by an oil lamp</note> according to the **degrees of fire** <note>original: *gradibus ignis*; the traditional four stages of increasing heat used in alchemical operations</note> until it has passed through all colors—about which a detailed account can be found in **Flamel** <note>Nicolas Flamel</note>, **Bernhard**, and many other **Philosophers**—until it reaches its **fixity** <note>original: *fixitæt*; the state where the substance is stable and no longer evaporates or changes when heated</note>. Then take it out of the glass and **ferment** it with **Gold**; that is, place it into soft, molten gold, and it will become entirely brittle. Then take this old, brittle gold, rub it small, add 2 parts of the **Female Seed** to it, and put it into a

<meta>catchword: ne (likely "neue" or "neben")</meta>

<summary>This page describes the precise proportions and methods for combining the "male" gold seed and "female" mercury seed. It dictates a 9:1 ratio, citing the authority of Alanus, and instructs the reader to heat the mixture until it achieves "fixity" and can be fermented with molten gold.</summary>

<keywords>proportions, Bernhardus Trevisanus, Alanus ab Insulis, Mercury seed, Gold seed, lamp-fire, degrees of fire, fixity, fermentation, brittle gold</keywords>

[in] a phial, let it pass through the colors again according to the **degrees of fire** <term>gradibus ignis: the four traditional stages of alchemical heat, often compared to the heat of the sun across the seasons</term> until it reaches the stage of the **Tincture** <note>the final concentrated substance capable of transmuting base metals</note>. Project <note>original: *tragt*; to cast a small amount of the substance onto molten metal</note> one part of this onto 100 parts of molten gold; it will then become brittle and like a glass.

This tincture now already **tinges** <term>tingiret: to transform or "dye" a base metal into a precious one</term> all metals—one part of it to 1000 parts—into the highest and finest gold.

### 20. To Extract the Seed from Silver Ore and Thereby Make a Tincture.

Take the beautiful red, transparent silver ore, which miners call **red silver ore** <note>original: *roth Guldens-Ertz*; scientifically known as pyrargyrite or proustite, these are ruby-red minerals prized by alchemists for their color</note>. Process it into a fine concentrate <term>Schlich: the fine, muddy ore residue left after washing and crushing</term> if it should not be entirely pure; grind it small and place it into a phial which is **hermetically sealed** <term>hermeticé sigilliret: a method of airtight sealing where the glass neck of a flask is melted shut</term>. Place this likewise in a **water bath** <term>Balneum: a vessel of water used to provide steady, gentle heat</term>, just as was described for the gold ore. Apply a gentle fire, and in the same manner as with the gold ore, the **seed** will rise to the top. Remove it and mix it with 2 parts of the **Female Seed** from the mercury ore. Replace it [in the vessel] and direct the fire likewise by means of a lamp. Initially, a blackness will manifest; when this has passed, the matter in the glass will begin to turn white again. At that point, it has already become a tincture. [With...]

### A Great Secret to Extract the Sulfur from Gold by Means of a Sweet Spirit of Salt <note>original: *Spiritum Salis dulcem*; likely a preparation of hydrochloric acid and alcohol or a neutralized acid</note>.

**Take** <term>Rec.: abbreviation for the Latin *Recipe*, "Take"</term> *niter* <term>Nitrum: saltpeter/potassium nitrate</term>. Dissolve it in a lye, filter it, evaporate it, and then let it crystallize. Dissolve it again in water, let it evaporate and crystallize <note>original: *anschliessen*</note> once more, and do this several times until it becomes so pure that it burns away completely. Let it flow in a *tubulated retort* <term>Retorta tabulata: a glass distillation vessel with a stoppered opening on the top for adding substances</term>, add charcoal dust <note>original: *Kohlen Gestübe*</note>, and it will pass over *with great force* <note>original: *cum magno impetu*</note>. There must be water in the receiver <note>original: *Vorlage*</note>, and it must have a little air [vent] the thickness of a quill. Separate the *watery residue* <term>phlegma: the thin, tasteless liquid separated during distillation</term>.

Mix 1 part of this *spirit* and 3 parts *spirit of salt* <term>Spiritum salis: hydrochloric acid</term> together and dissolve 1 part gold in it. Add the white *dregs* <term>feces: the solid residue left after dissolution</term>, then distill and frequently **redistill** <term>cohobirt: the alchemical practice of pouring a distilled liquid back over its solid residue and distilling again to concentrate the essence</term> it until it reaches an *oily consistency* <term>Olität</term>, until the water becomes weak

# XXI. To [Give] Gold its Sulfur, etc. <note>original: *XXI. Dem Gold seinen Schwefel 2c.*; "Sulfur" in alchemy refers to the combustible, active, or "soul" principle of a substance.</note>

### Sweet Spirit of Salt <note>original: *Spiritus Salis dulcis*; a neutralized or esterified version of hydrochloric acid, often made by reacting it with alcohol</note>

**Take** <term>Rec.: abbreviation for the Latin *Recipe*, "Take"</term> concentrated <term>Dephlegmirten: "dephlegmated," meaning the watery part (phlegm) has been removed through distillation</term> **spirit of salt** and pour into it one-and-a-half parts of **rectified spirit of wine** <term>Spiritum Vini Rectificatif.: high-proof distilled alcohol</term>. Draw it over strongly <note>distill it</note> so that nothing remains in the **bottom**. To that which has climbed over, pour half as much **spirit of wine** as it weighs; draw it over again, and do this a third time. Then let it **digest** in a **water bath** <term>Balneo: short for *Balneum Mariae*, a warm water bath used for gentle heating</term> for four weeks until it becomes entirely **sweet**.

### 22.

**An Extraction-Water, which His Serene Highness of M:::::: <note>The identity of "M" is likely a noble patron, common in alchemical manuscripts of this era.</note> received from a philosopher, by means of which he extracts the sulfur from gold, and with the same, in an easy manner, transforms silver into gold.**

Then distill this from a sand-bath; the retort is also covered on top with sand. First, give it a gentle fire for 4 hours, then strengthen the fire, and finally use a strong fire until the heating chamber <term>Capelle: the cupel or the furnace chamber</term> glows. When it has cooled, pour the distilled water back onto the "dead head" <term>Caput Mortuum: the solid, exhausted residue remaining after distillation</term>, namely the remains of the salts...

# XXII. Extraction-Water.

[Take a] crucible that is glowing [red-hot] and throw the following powder onto it:

**Take:** Glass gall <term>Glass gall: also known as sandiver; a salty scum that forms on the surface of molten glass</term> and white crude Tartar <term>Tartarum Crudum: unrefined potassium tartrate collected from the insides of wine casks</term>, 1 ounce of each. 5 parts of Venetian Crystal glass <note>original: *Vitr. Venet. Crystall.*; a high-quality, clear glass from Venice</note>, 3 ounces.

Pulverize and mix them together; throw it all together into a crucible onto the mass. Cover it and let it melt well; then, once poured out and the crucible broken open, the **regulus** <term>regulus: the metallic button or lump found at the bottom of a crucible after smelting</term> refined <note>original: *abgetrieben*; the process of cupellation to separate precious metals from base metals</note> with 3 parts lead, then you have the very best gold.

This **Sulphur of Gold** <term>Gold Schwefel: the alchemical "soul" or color-giving principle extracted from gold</term> can also be **fixed** <term>fixed: rendered stable and resistant to heat</term> on its own in two glasses or urinal-shaped vessels placed one over the other, through constant turning; this occurs in 24 hours, for it becomes so volatile from the **solvent** <term>menstruo: a liquid used to dissolve or extract substances</term> that through several **cohobations** <term>cohobation: the repeated redistillation of a solvent back onto the solid matter from which it was distilled</term> it passes over with the lead <note>The symbol ♄ represents Saturn or Lead</note>.

### Reduction of the White Body into the Sun.

Or melted with the **regulus of antimony**, blown off, and refined; thus it colors itself again and protects the absorbed Sulphur on the **cupel** <term>capelle: a shallow, porous ceramic dish used to refine precious metals by absorbing oxidized base metals</term>.

...putrefy <note>original: *putrificiren*; to let a substance decompose or ferment to break it down</note>; then distill it and cohobate <term>cohobation: the process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid residue and re-distilling it</term> it several times. ->23.<-

**How to make Iron as soft as Lead.**

Putrefy urine and distill a spirit from it; rectify this spirit <note>original: *rectificire*; to purify a liquid through repeated distillation</note>. Afterward, extract the salt from the **Dead Head** <term>Caput Mortuum: the "worthless" solid remains left in a flask after the liquid has been distilled away</term>. Calcine this salt until it is white <note>original: *ad albedinem*</note>. Then take 8 loth <note>loth: a historical Germanic unit of weight; 8 loth is approximately 4 ounces</note> of this salt and dissolve it in 1 pound of the spirit. Next, heat the iron until it glows, and quench it in the aforementioned spirit. Do this several times and the iron will become completely soft; however, as soon as you quench it again in common water, it will become hard once more.

->24.<-

**A beautiful Gradation for Gold.**

Beat copper that has been made **from Mars** <term>ex Marte: copper that has been precipitated or treated with iron (Mars), often considered to have special properties</term> into thin sheets. Cement them with cinnabar <note>original: *Berg-Zinober*; natural mercury sulfide</note> three times until the sheets become brittle and crumbly. Put the sheets aside and sublime the cinnabar with a strong fire in a glass vessel <note>sublimation: heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again at the top of the vessel</note>. A beautiful red powder will remain at the bottom. Cast a portion of this powder onto pure gold while it is in flux <note>in flux: in a molten, liquid state</note> and let them melt together; this will make the gold very beautiful and high in color <note>original: *hoch*; referring to the richness of the gold's hue and its purity</note>.

# XXV. Making Gold into a Tincture.

->25.<-

### To make gold into a tincture through cementation.

Take Hungarian Vitriol <term>vitriol: a metal sulfate; "Hungarian" vitriol was often a high-quality copper sulfate</term> and brick-dust <note>original: *Ziegel-Mehl*; used as a carrier for the chemicals</note>, 3 loth <note>loth: a historical weight; 3 loth is approximately 1.5 ounces</note> of each. Take 2 loth of salt. Soak these together with urine. Then take 1 loth of gold and beat it into thin sheets <note>original: *lamellire*</note>. Arrange the powder and the sheets layer upon layer <note>original: *stratum super stratum*</note>. Let it cement <term>cementation: a process where a metal is heated with powdered chemicals to alter its properties without melting it first</term> for a few hours. Afterward, apply a strong melting-heat, and the gold will become soft and brittle, allowing itself to be ground like a saffron <note>original: *Saffran*; here referring to "Saffron of Gold," a fine reddish-yellow powder</note> on a grinding stone. Afterward, soak the gold powder with the aforementioned moistened powder; let it flow [in the fire] again. Then apply this to silver, and you will see wonders. For the gold has been turned into a tincture <term>tincture: an alchemical substance believed to have the power to "color" or transmute base metals into gold</term>.

### 27.

## Secret Panacea to be used in all acute fevers, and wherever other sudden ailments are found.

Take 8 ounces of Hungarian antimony <note>antimony was a favorite mineral of 17th-century chemists for both transmutation and medicine</note>. Pulverize it and mix in 1 pound of rasped deer horn <note>original: *Hirsch-Horn*; hartshorn was commonly used in medicine as a source of ammonia and calcium</note>. Divide it into 2 parts. Place one part into a melting crucible and let it become glowing hot. Then stir it with iron hooks for as long as it takes for the material to settle at the bottom. Then take the second part and add it to the first in the crucible. Continue stirring until the material settles in the same manner and no longer smokes. Then let it cool and grind it on a marble mortar into a fine powder; keep it in a dry place. The dose is 16, 20, to 24 grains <term>grain: a tiny unit of weight, roughly 0.06 grams</term> taken early in the morning in elderberry juice; afterwards...

### Secret for Refining Empyreumatic Oils.

<term>Take</term> <note>original: *Rec.*, an abbreviation for the Latin *recipe*</note> thinly beaten gold; dissolve it in *Aqua Regis* <term>Aqua Regis: Latin for "King's Water"; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids used to dissolve gold</term>, precipitate it with *Spirit of Salmiac* <term>Spirit of Salmiac: an old term for a solution of ammonia</term>, edulcorate <term>edulcorate: to wash a chemical precipitate with water to remove soluble salts or acids</term> the precipitate, and abstract <term>abstract: to distill or draw off a liquid</term> about 8 or 9 parts of an empyreumatic oil <term>empyreumatic oils: oils produced by heating organic substances at very high temperatures, often resulting in a burnt or smoky smell</term> over one part of this gold preparation. In a single operation, the oil will become brighter, clearer, and more pleasant than could otherwise be achieved through many other operations. From the residues, one can collect all of the gold again in the proper manner and reduce it back to its metal state. And who knows what other hidden power gold—which strengthens all hearts and eyes—imparts to the empyreumatic oil in this operation? <note>In early modern medicine, gold was associated with the Sun and believed to have restorative powers for the heart and the sense of sight.</note>
