[Sal ammo]niac into flowers <term>Flowers of Sal Ammoniac: A white crystalline powder obtained through the sublimation of ammonium chloride.</term>; these they dissolve in a strong **lye**, **digest** <term>Digestion: The process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to encourage chemical change.</term> them together for several weeks, and then draw the moisture away from it. Pour a truly good **Spirit of Salt** <term>Spirit of Salt: An old name for hydrochloric acid.</term> upon it, draw it off again, and obtain with a strong fire a **Sublimated Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium Sublimatum*</note>, which they then **revivify** <term>Revivification: The alchemical process of restoring a metal to its original liquid or metallic state from a chemical compound.</term> in the usual manner <note>original: *more solito*</note>. This is a good process, but one must master the manual techniques well.

Some dissolve the **Regulus** <term>Regulus: The metallic mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting; in this context, the metallic form of antimony.</term> in a certain **Aqua Regis** <term>Aqua Regis: "Royal Water," a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids used to dissolve noble metals.</term> specially prepared for this purpose through a particular manual technique; they then, after the solution has occurred, pour in a special liquid, digest it for a time, sublimate and rectify it, and proceed with it in all things as in the previous process.

Finally, there are some who mix the Regulus well with **Sal Ammoniac** and distill it **via Retort** <term>Retort: A glass or earthenware vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</term>, through which they obtain a beautiful **Sublimate** and **Cinnabar** <term>Cinnabar: Traditionally mercury sulfide, here referring to a red mineral-like compound produced during the reaction.</term>. They mix both of these with **Salt of Tartar** <term>Salt of Tartar: Potassium carbonate, often used as a flux or to help release mercury from its ores.</term> and **Hammer-Scale** <term>Hammer-Scale: Iron oxide flakes that fall from hot iron when it is forged.</term> through an advantageous manual technique, and drive the **Mercury** over via Retort. They thus obtain from 1 pound of Regulus, 6 **Loth** <note>A "Loth" was a German unit of weight equivalent to approximately 15 grams or 1/32nd of a pound.</note> of Mercury. This method is practicable, but the manual techniques must be carefully observed, otherwise it will hardly succeed.

## An Experiment which yields Silver and Gold with Profit.

Afterward, grind it small again and cement <note>cement: To heat a solid in a powdered reagent to change its properties without melting it.</note> it once more for two hours as before. After this, melt it with white flux to a **regulus** <term>Regulus: The metallic button that settles at the bottom of a crucible after smelting; literally "little king."</term>, mixed with a bit of the **stone of the three** <note>original: *Lapide de tribus*; likely a specific alchemical compound involving three components like sulfur, salt, and mercury.</note>. Pour both solutions together, draw off <note>original: *abstrahire*; evaporate or distill</note> the water, and melt it together. ### True Graduating and Fixing Oil

<note>"Graduating" refers to the process of improving the color, purity, or "grade" of a metal; "Fixing" refers to making a volatile substance stable under high heat.</note>

First, one makes a Butter of Antimony <note>original: "Butyrum Antimonii," antimony trichloride, a chemical with a thick, buttery consistency</note> from equal parts <note>original: "ana"</note> of antimony ore and mercury sublimate. As is customary, this must be distilled three times over its own cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note>. Furthermore, take red talc and Bohemian garnets, 1 part of each; mix three parts of common sulfur into it; calcine <note>heat to a high temperature to reduce to powder</note> this according to the art <note>referring to alchemical skill</note> until the sulfur is completely burnt off; extract the tincture with strong Royal Water <note>original: "Aqua Regis," a powerful mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acid capable of dissolving gold</note>; draw this off from the sulfur of the talc and garnets; the sulfur is then washed <note>original: "edulcoriret"</note> and dried; then pour over it the thickness of a finger of the mentioned Butter of Antimony; let it digest <note>steep in gentle heat</note> together for 3 days and nights; then distill and cohobate <note>the process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid residue and re-distilling</note> it as often as necessary until the "butter" becomes as red as a ruby, which usually happens on the third time; finally, one must apply a strong fire until all moisture is driven out; thus the Graduating Oil is ready to transmute prepared silver <note>original: "Lunam," or "The Moon," the alchemical name for silver</note> into gold by the described method of digestion.

### The shortest and easiest way to separate gold from silver in flux.

Whoever wishes to separate gold from silver by the dry way <note>a metallurgical process using heat and melting rather than liquid acids</note> must well understand the nature of sulfur and its properties regarding metals,

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>✻ ( 0 ) ✻</header>

...for it attacks all metals and turns them into **slag** <note>original: "Schlacken," the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting</note>; however, only a certain *quantity*, and one metal more readily than another; and upon this the *foundation* of separation is based. Since one only wants to use this separation for gold and silver, it is enough to know how *sulfur* <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> behaves toward gold, toward silver, toward the *antimony regulus* <term>Regulum Antimonii: the metallic form of antimony, often found as a heavy lump at the bottom of a crucible after smelting</term> and toward iron—or in its place, toward copper, etc. When pure gold is in flux <note>in a molten, liquid state</note>, and one throws *sulfur* upon it, the *sulfur* burns away and does not attack the gold. On the other hand, when silver is mixed with the gold, it attacks the silver and transforms it into black slag, which floats above the gold; and thus it separates the gold from the silver.

Meanwhile, if one lets *potash* <note>original: "Potasche," an alkaline salt (potassium carbonate) used as a flux to help materials melt</note> melt with the *sulfur*, it attacks the gold as well and turns it into slag. Without *potash*, it does indeed attack the silver; however, because it smokes and fumes heavily, it robs the silver <note>meaning some of the silver is lost to the fumes or becomes chemically unrecoverable</note>, and it is difficult to *reduce* <note>original: "reduciren," to restore a metal from its chemical compound or slag back to a pure metallic state</note> it back into silver. However, if one melts half a pound of sulfur <note>original: "Schwefel"</note> and half a pound of *potash*, it attacks and dissolves half a pound of gold—provided that one brings the *potash* quickly into flux with the *sulfur* so that not too much *sulfur* evaporates; otherwise, it will not attack as much, though one can help by adding more *sulfur*. If one adds half a pound of *antimony regulus* to the gold thus in flux, the *sulfur* lets the gold drop, so that it flows together into a *regulus* <note>a solid metallic mass</note>; conversely, the *antimony regulus* is dissolved and thus lost; if one adds...

Should the <term>Regulus</term> <note>The metallic button or lump that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting.</note> happen to be too large, add a little more sulfur and potash to it, and it will become smaller; however, if no button is found at all, add 1 or 2 <term>Loth</term> <note>A historical German unit of weight, approximately 1/32nd of a pound or 14.6 grams.</note> of iron, or even better, metallic antimony.

The most precise method of parting, which concentrates the gold from the silver most effectively, is achieved when one adds antimony along with the sulfur; for the metallic antimony acts as a medium between the gold and the silver. Accordingly, for every <term>Mark</term> <note>A unit of weight for precious metals, roughly 234 grams.</note> of silver that contains a half-ducat’s worth of gold, take 8 Loth of sulfur, 8 Loth of common antimony, and 16 Loth of potash; a button of about 4, 6, or 8 Loth will then settle out, depending on how the fire is managed. If the button weighs 8 Loth, it contains roughly 4 Loth of silver and 3 3/5 Loth of metallic antimony; for this reason, one may well add another 4 Loth of sulfur and 4 Loth of potash to this button in the melting crucible, so that the button might be reduced to weigh 4 Loth.

This button is then refined by air-blast <note>original: "verbläset", referring to the process of cupellation or using a blast of air to oxidize impurities.</note>; because antimony is present, the gold remains almost entirely pure. The silver remains trapped in the <term>Slag</term> <note>The stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting.</note>; therefore, let the slag flow well in the melting crucible and add half a pound of iron nails or iron filings to it. Let it flow well for three-quarters of an hour, then pour it out, and you will have your silver back again. Should there be too little recovered, let the slag flow once more and add more iron to it; in this way, everything will settle and <term>precipitate</term> <note>original: "præcipitiren", the process of a substance settling out of a melt to the bottom of the vessel.</note> in the purest manner. Test the silver, and it will be pure. If it has become heavier, it can be <term>cupelled</term> <note>original: "abtreiben", a refining process where ores are heated under a high-pressure blast of air to separate noble metals from base metals like lead.</note> or otherwise used...

### 40.

### The **Most True [Process] via Oil of Antimony** by the wet path.

Make a **fixed red Glass of Antimony** <note>original: *Vitrum Antimonii rubrum fixum*; a glass-like substance created by roasting antimony ore</note>, grind it small, and pour **Oil of Salt** <note>original: *Oleum Salis*; hydrochloric acid</note> over it. Extract its fixed **Sulphur** <note>the active, combustible principle believed to exist within minerals</note> or its yellow essence. Pour it off and pour more **Oil of Salt** onto it; do this repeatedly until it will extract nothing more. Then distill it in a **water bath** <note>original: *B. M.* or *Balneum Mariae*</note>, so that a **coagulated matter** <note>original: *coagulirte Materia*</note> and a red oil remain at the **bottom** <note>original: *fundo*</note>. Afterward, distill this from the sand <note>a sand bath, which provides higher, more even heat than water</note> using a low glass vessel or **retort**, and catch it as it comes over. Thus, you have a red **Oil of Antimony**.

Then, dissolve the **Quinte Essence of Mercury** <note>the highly refined core or "fifth essence" of mercury</note>, as previously reported, in **Oil of Salt**, and pour the solution into 6 loth <note>a German unit of weight, roughly 1/2 ounce or 15 grams</note> of **Oil of Antimony**. Let it digest in a **water bath** for 3 days and nights. Afterward, distill it from the sand **by means of an alembic** <note>original: *per Alembicum*; a classic distillation apparatus</note> using a low glass vessel, so that it rises over the helm <note>the cap or "head" of the still</note>. If any good material remains in the **dregs** <note>original: *fecibus*</note>, pour the distilled **Oil** back onto it. Let the **earth** <note>the solid residue</note> digest as before and distill once more, so that whatever good was still in the **earth** <note>original: *terra*</note> rises over. Thus, you have the true **Tinging Oil** <note>original: *Oleum tingens*; an oil capable of transmuting metals</note>, which tinges **Silver** <note>original: *Lunam*, literally "the Moon"</note> into gold, **as follows** <note>original: *ut sequitur*</note>:

Make an **Aquafortis** <note>literally "strong water," usually nitric acid</note> as is customary; in it, dissolve 24 loth of silver. Into this, pour one loth of this **Oil**. Let it stand in a comfortable warmth in a **water bath** day and night; thus, 6 loth of constant, good gold will precipitate. Pour off the water and reduce it <note>melt the precipitate back into solid metal</note>. This water is then better than before for the digestion and **imbibing** of the **Oil of Sulphur**.

**Note: From the Mercury**, for every 4 loth, 1 loth of gold falls out; that is 8 loth of constant gold from 1 pound, [holding true] in all tests.

## 2.
## How one should corrupt gold and bring it into its first matter, that is, into Living Mercury <note>original Latin: Mercurium Vivum; quicksilver or liquid mercury considered to be in its primal, 'living' state</note>.

First, prepare the <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride, a salt used as a flux in alchemy</note> as follows: Take 1 or 2 pounds of pure sal ammoniac, and take an equal weight of <term>rock salt</term> <note>original Latin: sal gemmæ</note>; grind both very finely together, and <term>sublime</term> <note>the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again upon cooling to purify it</note> it with a strong fire; it will first sublime into a beautiful yellow. Take out that same sublimed sal ammoniac very cleanly, and add to it again an equal weight of rock salt as the first time, and sublime it just as before. Perform this sublimation 6 times, and each time with new rock salt; thus the sal ammoniac will become very sharp and a beautiful yellow, like a meadow flower. Then it is ready; guard it well so that the air does not draw out its power or cause it to dissolve.

### Thereafter, make a Quintessence as follows:

℞. Take the very best wine you can find, or a good strong brandy made from grapes, and distill it as strongly as possible, so that from 12 measures hardly one measure remains. When you have thus properly <term>rectified</term> <note>refined or purified a liquid through repeated distillation</note> several measures, soak a clean little cloth in it and light it. If it burns away completely, then it is correct and is the <term>Quintessence of wine</term> <note>original Latin: Q. E. vini; high-proof ethanol</note>. Guard it well so its power does not evaporate.

# Now calcine the Tartar in this way:

Take 8 or 10 pounds of white tartar <note>original: "weiſſen Weinſtein"; the potassium bitartrate sediment found in wine barrels</note> /

thick / and reverberate it with strong fire
flames / for 7 or 8 hours / after that turn
the bottom to the top / so it will become throughout as
white as snow / put this calcined <term>calcined: reduced to powder through intense heat</term> tartar
into a good, sturdy crucible / cover it tightly / and
place it in a wind furnace and let it flow with strong
fire like water. When it has thus
flowed / then pour it into a mortar / and let
it cool / then grind it small on a warm
grinding stone / and keep it in a warm place
so that it does not dissolve <note>original: "ſolvire"; the processed tartar is hygroscopic and would absorb moisture from the air if not kept warm and dry</note>.

### Prepare the Gold for this work as follows:

If you wish to *precipitate* <note>to cause a substance to separate from a solution as a solid</note> the *mercury of gold* <note>original: "Mercurium Solis"; "Sol" or the Sun was the alchemical symbol for gold</note> with the *mercury of lead* <note>original: "Mercurio Saturni"; "Saturn" was the alchemical symbol for lead</note> as mentioned above, you can obtain a tincture in the same manner.

## To Prepare a *Mercury of Gold* in Another Manner.

Take as much gold as you wish, dissolve it in *aqua regia* <note>original: "Aqua Regis," or "royal water"; a powerful mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids used to dissolve noble metals</note>, and draw the *aqua regia* off it again in a *water bath* <note>original: "MB." for *Balneum Mariae*; a vessel of hot water used to heat laboratory glassware gently</note>; pour more *aqua regia* onto it and draw it off in the *water bath*; do this a third time. Then pour a good *spirit of wine* <note>original: "Spiritum vini"; concentrated ethanol or brandy</note> over it and draw it off three times in the *water bath* as well. After this, take twice as much *salt of tartar* <note>original: "Salis Tartari"; potassium carbonate, often used as a flux or purifying agent</note> as there is gold—judged by eye and not by weight—and you may moisten it a little with a few drops of *aqua regia*. Rub them together, place them in a glass flask, and set it in a sand-capel <note>a sand bath used to distribute heat evenly around a glass vessel</note>. On the first day, give it gentle heat for 12 hours; on the second day, a stronger heat for another 12 hours; on the third day, a stronger heat again for 12 hours. Let it cool, and you will find the *mercury of gold* clinging to the helm <note>the "head" or top part of a still where vapors condense</note> and inside the beak of the helm like a powder. Take distilled vinegar, turn the helm upside down, pour the vinegar into it, and rub it back and forth with your fingers; thus the *mercury* will run together at the bottom into small living grains <note>this refers to "living" or liquid metallic mercury</note>. *Precipitate* this however you wish.

## To Make a *Living Mercury* from the Crystals of Lead.

Take the *mercurial salt* <note>original: "Salis Mercurialis"</note>, which the *Hermeti-* <note>The text breaks off here, likely referring to the "Hermetic philosophers" or practitioners of the alchemical arts</note>

mercury <note>original: "mercurium"</note> is the Eagle <note>The "Eagle" in alchemy typically refers to a volatile substance, often sal ammoniac, that has the power to "lift" or sublimate other minerals</note> which carries gold, silver, metals, and minerals with it to the highest mountains. Dissolve it in spring water, filter it, and turn it back into a salt <note>the symbol 🜔 represents salt</note>. Of this salt, take as much as you wish. Boil eggs, remove the yolks, and place this volatile salt into the hollows. Set them in a damp cellar and let the salt dissolve into a liquid <note>this process is called deliquescence</note>. Pour this liquid over the Salt of Saturn <note>original: "Sal ♄i"; lead acetate</note> so that it covers it by two fingers' breadth. Place it in a vial plugged with paper upon warm ashes in an athanor <note>a furnace designed to provide steady, uniform heat</note>. Let it stand for 14 days or longer. Then distill the water from it in a flask, and sublimate the remainder—gently at first, then increasingly stronger. In this way, the Saturn <note>Lead</note> sublimates upward with the Eagle. When rubbed with vinegar and salt <note>🜔</note>, it will soon become "alive" <note>meaning it turns into liquid, running mercury</note>. This mercury <note>☿io</note> is sought after by many artists <note>alchemists</note> who believe they can achieve much with it in *chrysopoeia* <note>the art of making gold</note>; however, without the King of Metals <note>gold</note>, they will achieve little. Otherwise, when it is precipitated, it can accomplish much and work wonders in medicine.

### Another way. <note>original: "Alius modus"</note>

Take Saturn <note>lead</note> that has been precipitated with salt <note>🜔</note> like silver <note>☽</note>, potash, and quicklime <note>original: "calc. viv." or *calx viva*</note>, 2 lots <note>a "Lot" was a unit of weight approximately equal to half an ounce</note> of each. Put it in a flask and drive it with a strong fire, and a powder will settle. Reduce this with vinegar, and you will obtain a running mercury <note>☿ium currentem</note>.

### Item. <note>meaning "Also" or "Likewise"</note>

Take 2 lots of the calx <note>a powder produced by roasting a metal</note> of Saturn precipitated with salt <note>🜔</note>, 2 lots of quicklime, and 3 quintlein <note>a "Quentchen" or "Quintlein" was a small weight, about 1/4 of a lot</note> each of salt of tartar <note>potassium carbonate</note> and common salt <note>🜔</note>.

## 4. To make a Mercury of Silver. <note>original: "Mercurium Lunae"</note>

Take 8 lots of Calx of Silver <note>original: "Calcem Lunae"; silver that has been dissolved and precipitated</note> that has been precipitated with salt, 2 lots of flowers of sal ammoniac <note>the purified, powdered form of ammonium chloride</note> which have been sublimated with rock salt <note>original: "Sal Gemma"</note>, and 2 lots of alkaline salt. Add 2 quentchen each of Salt of Tartar and volatile salt of urine <note>ammonium carbonate</note>, and 4 lots of highly rectified spirit of wine <note>concentrated ethanol</note>. Rub them together and place the mixture in "putrefaction" <note>a stage of slow digestion or rotting in gentle heat</note> for 3 to 4 weeks. Then, drive it out of a glass retort <term>Retort: A glass vessel with a long, curved neck used for distilling liquids</term>, and it will yield a beautiful running mercury.

### 6.
## To make a Mercury of Mars.

℞. <note>Recipe</note> Two pounds of sal ammoniac <note>original: Salmiac; ammonium chloride</note>, 1 pound of saltpeter <note>original: Nitrum; potassium nitrate</note>, rub them well together; then mix it with 9 pounds of clay <term>Leimen; likely a potter's clay used as a base for distillation</term> and make small balls out of it. When these are dry, place them in a ceramic retort, attach a large receiver <note>original: Vorlage; a glass vessel used to collect the distillate</note>, and drive the spirit <term>Spiritum; an acidic vapor or liquid distillate</term> out of them very cautiously. Then pour this spirit onto iron plates; the spirit will constantly eat away at the plates, and a running mercury <term>Mercurius; liquid quicksilver or the supposed metallic essence of the iron</term> will be found at the bottom. Take this away; dry the sludge that has settled at the bottom and mix it with 2 parts iron filings. Put it into a retort and drive with a strong fire, and even more mercury will pass over into the water that must be in the receiver. From 1 pound of iron, one obtains nearly 15 to 16 loth <note>a historical unit of weight; about 1/2 ounce or 15 grams</note> of running mercury.

### 7.
## To make a Mercury of Venus.

Take equal parts of copper dross <note>original: Kupffer-Schlacken; the waste product of smelting</note> and sulfur; mix well together. Let them calcine <term>calciniren; to heat a substance to high temperatures in the air to oxidize it</term> in a pot for three or four hours, and it will become a crocus <note>original: Crocus; a name for a reddish-brown metallic powder or oxide</note>. Then weigh this crocus again, and mix into 1 pound of it three or four loth of sulfur. Put it in a pot placed on its side between two bricks; make a gentle charcoal fire beneath it and let the sulfur slowly evaporate <note>original: verriechen; literally "to smell away," meaning to fume off</note>. However, you must stir constantly with a small copper or iron rod for a quarter of an hour. Finally, you may strengthen the fire so that all the sulfur <note>original: Sulphur</note> goes away; take it out

### To make a tincture from the ore of antimony, by means of which one part may transmute one thousand parts of silver into the finest gold.

Take 1 pound of antimony ore <term>Mineram Antimonii</term> and 2 pounds of mercury sublimate <term>Mercurii sublimati; mercuric chloride, a highly toxic corrosive sublimate</term>; mix them well together and place them in a cellar for 10 days, and it will become a silver-colored white mash. Pour fresh spring water or distilled rainwater upon it, and a white powder will precipitate <note>This white powder is known as the Powder of Algaroth</note>, but the water will become acidic. and distill it; first the phlegm <term>Phlegma; the watery, non-volatile part of a distillate</term> passes over, followed by a spirit <term>Spiritus; a volatile substance</term>, and finally a strong water <term>▽; alchemical symbol for water</term> with the colors of a rainbow, which is very sharp <note>original: scharff; meaning acidic or corrosive</note>, and which some call the Spirit of the Philosophers <note>original: Spiritum Philosophorum</note>; save this.

NB. Instead of distilling it, the Count simply let this water evaporate until the residue <note>original: Residuum</note> took on the color of yellow sulfur; he then poured this over the antimony ore <term>Minera Antimonii</term> and let it stand without fire.

Pour this water onto fresh ore and let it stand in a flask for five weeks without fire; the water will turn a deep yellow. Pass this water through paper <note>used as a filter</note> so that it becomes pure and clear, then draw it off using a water bath <term>per Balneum; heating a vessel inside a container of hot water for gentle, even heat</term>; this will yield a beautiful, deep red extraction. Pour distilled rain or well water over this again, and you will obtain a white powder; save and dry it. From the remaining water, however, produce a vitriol <term>Vitriol; a crystalline metal sulfate</term> and purify it many times. Then take 1 part of the white powder and 2 parts of the vitriol; mix them together, place them in a phial <term>Phiole; a small glass vessel</term>, seal it tightly <note>likely hermetically sealing the glass with heat</note>, and set it aside. It will then resolve itself, turning black, white, and red <note>These represent the traditional stages of the Great Work: nigredo, albedo, and rubedo</note>. And when it has reached perfection, one part will not only transmute <term>tingiret; to "tinge" or transform a base metal into gold or silver</term> a thousand parts, but it can also be multiplied in such a way that if you take one part of this tincture and three parts of the previous vitriol, and set it aside again for three months to be fixed <term>figiren; to make a volatile substance solid or stable under heat</term>, it will then transmute even more than before—and this can be repeated until its power to transmute is countless.

NB. In my notebooks <term>Collectaneis; a collection of notes or excerpts</term>, I found how, in the crystallization of metals, the Spirit of Tartar <term>Spiritum Tartari; a potassium carbonate solution derived from wine tartar</term> had been used for precipitation in its proper weight, no more and no less. I imagine the weight is correct when it no longer effervesces <note>original: zusammen brauset; literally "fizzes together," indicating a chemical reaction reaching neutrality</note>. In my own practice <term>Praxi; practical application or experiment</term>, I let the "Schweitzerl." <note>likely a reference to a specific chemist or a regional variation</note> Spirit of Tartar drip into the Spirit of the Philosophers <note>original: Spiritum Philosophorum; a high-grade alchemical solvent or acid</note> until it stopped fizzing; then, after evaporation, the vitriol <term>Vitriol; a crystalline metal sulfate</term> crystallized.

->9.<-
### An Augmentation of Gold & Silver.
<note>original: Augmentum Solis & Lunae; "Increase of the Sun and Moon," referring to the alchemical process of increasing the quantity of precious metals.</note>

First, make a powder from one part silver, 4 parts mercury <note>original: Mercurio</note>, and 12 parts lead. Specifically: let everything flow together on a large, thin scorifier <term>Treib-Scherben; a shallow clay dish used in assaying to separate metals</term> for 3 days and nights under the muffle <term>Muffel; a ceramic chamber in a furnace that protects the material from direct contact with fuel</term>, and afterwards let it stand until it becomes a brown-red powder. Put this into a crucible <term>Schmelz-Tiegel; a vessel used for melting substances at high temperatures</term>, place it in a small coal fire, and let it stay in there until it transforms into a light-grey ash, which happens in about a day. 

Then, place lead on a scorifier under the muffle, and when the lead "drives" <note>original: treibt; the process of lead oxidizing and being absorbed during cupellation</note>, put a little tin on it so that it bursts open <note>original: börstet; likely referring to the rapid oxidation or "spitting" of the metal</note>; then take a small assay-spoon <term>Probier-Löffelchen</term> full of the aforementioned light-grey powder and pour it on top—specifically on the "burst" tin on the scorifier—and let it stand until the tin in the lead becomes a delicate slag. Then pour out the scorifier and place the slag with fresh lead onto the cupel <term>Capelle; a porous ceramic cup used to separate precious metals from base metals</term>. Let it "drive" until the lead is gone; then one part of the tin and mercury <note>original: Mercurius</note> remains as a stone <note>meaning a solid metallic bead or residue</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the precise use of Spirit of Tartar to neutralize acids and describes a metallurgical process for 'augmenting' silver using lead, mercury, and tin.</summary>

Turn this stone into slag again with lead <term>Bley</term> as before, and apply the light-grey powder onto it; then the tin-stone <note>original: Zinn-Stein; likely referring to cassiterite or a specific alchemical preparation of tin</note> becomes even more fluid, so that it can merge into gold and silver. Reduce this slag with a strong fire into a **regulus** <term>Könige; literally "king," the metallic mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible</term>, and turn it back into a stone or powder on the **scorifier** <term>Treib-Scherben; a shallow clay dish used in assaying</term> and **cupel** <term>Capelle; a small, porous bowl used to separate precious metals from lead</term>. Cast 1 part of this onto five parts of gold or silver in a bright molten flow, and let it flow well for another hour; it augments gold and silver, etc.

### 10.
### How to make good melting and refining hearths, or pre-crucibles.

Mix with white clay (from which glassmakers prepare their **glass-pots** <term>Glas-Häfen; large ceramic vessels used for melting glass</term>) or another firm clay that has been well-washed and cleaned of all sand, an equal amount of **calcined** <term>calcined; heated to high temperatures to remove volatile substances</term> and finely ground Venetian or other good talc, or a calcined **spar** <term>Spaad; a mineral like feldspar or fluorspar</term>. With beer (in which some salt has been dissolved), make it into a mass of proper consistency, and from this, make crucibles or hearths beaten into molds.

->Also.<-

Make a mass from 2 parts glue <note>likely a thick binder or animal glue</note>, 3 parts crucible earth, 1 part pulverized charcoal, and three parts **iron scales** <term>Eisen-Sieter; the flaky oxidation that falls off iron during forging</term> by weight <note>original: ratione ponderis</note> with **wine dregs** <term>Wein-Häfen; the sediment or lees from wine</term>, and beat hearths from it.

->Also.<-

Take two parts fine white **potter's clay** <term>Haffner Thon</term>, and one part each <note>original: ana; a term meaning "of each"</note> of brick dust and fine charcoal dust. Make a mass out of it with beer and beat it into molds.

[...the] third part of the <term>Aqua Regis</term> <note>original: "Aqua Regis"; literally "Royal Water," a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note> behind it); note here that you should not coat the retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation</note> too heavily [with sand], but rather leave a flat spot on top so that one can easily see inside to observe how much of the Aqua Regis is passing over. Then let it cool, and pour the distilled Aqua Regis back onto it; distill it again until it reaches an oily consistency <term>Olität</term> <note>referring to a thick, syrupy state of a substance after distillation</note>. Repeat this process of distilling off and pouring back 5 or 6 times. If the Aqua Regis becomes too weak, add fresh [acid] and draw it off until it reaches the oily state. Then pour onto this oily substance a well-purified <term>Oil of Vitriol</term> <note>original: "Oleum vitrioli"; concentrated sulfuric acid</note>, enough to cover it considerably. Then place it in a <term>sand-capelle</term> <note>a sand bath used to distribute heat evenly around a glass vessel</note>; seal the retort well with lute [sealant], and cover it well with sand in the bath. Give it a gentle fire at first, then increase the heat gradually, so that the retort finally glows for 1 or 2 hours. In this way, the **Gold** <note>original Latin: "Solis" (of the Sun), the alchemical symbol for gold</note> passes over with the Oil of Vitriol; likewise, it passes into the flask through the alembic head. If it has not all passed over, pour more fresh Oil of Vitriol onto the remaining gold until everything has been drawn over.

Place this solution of Gold and Vitriol into the <term>B. M.</term> <note>Balneum Mariae, or "Mary's Bath"—a gentle water bath</note> and draw off the <term>phlegma</term> <note>the watery, inactive portion of a distillate</note> until you have 3 or 4 **Loth** <note>a unit of weight, roughly 15-17 grams per Loth</note> of Oil of Vitriol for every one Loth of gold; this will be a thick liquid. Use this liquid to precipitate **Silver** <note>original Latin: "Lunam" (the Moon), the alchemical symbol for silver</note>; namely: dissolve the silver in strong <term>Aquafort</term> <note>original: "Aquafort" or Aqua Fortis; nitric acid</note>. When this is done, pour 5 or 6 drops of the Oil of Gold and Vitriol into it, and it will precipitate the silver as a completely brown [powder]. Then let it stand for a while until it has settled; then pour in more of this Oil as before. Continue this until no more silver will precipitate in the nitric acid.

# How to Make the Oil of <term>Vitriol</term> for this Precipitation.

Take <term>vitriol</term> <note>iron or copper sulfate</note>, <term>sulfur</term>, and <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "Antimonium"; likely the mineral stibnite</note>, an equal amount of each / pounded clean / and cemented <note>heated in a dry state</note> together in a crucible with a gentle fire for one hour / until the <term>sulfur</term> <note>original Latin: "Sulphur"</note> burns off / then again add fresh <term>sulfur</term> to the <term>vitriol</term> / however, the <term>vitriol</term> must first be <term>calcined</term> <note>heated to a high temperature to drive off volatile matter</note> to redness / place it back on the fire / let the <term>sulfur</term> burn away / do this 5 or 6 times / so the <term>vitriol</term> and <term>sulfur</term> become fixed <note>made resistant to heat, preventing them from evaporating or changing further</note> / you may always include the <term>antimony</term> with the <term>vitriol</term>. When you have let it burn 5 or 6 times / the <term>sulfur</term>, <term>vitriol</term>, and <term>antimony</term> are fixed. Onto this fixed **matter** <note>original Latin: "Materiam"</note> pour a simple water or distilled <term>vinegar</term> / or the <term>phlegm</term> <note>the watery, less potent distillate</note> of <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>nitric acid</note> / so that it becomes blood-red / then pour it off and add more / do this until it no longer draws out any redness / then pour the **solutions** <note>original Latin: "Solutiones"</note> together and filter them / then draw off the <term>phlegm</term> from it in a <term>Bain-Marie</term> <note>original: "B. M."; a gentle water bath</note> until it reaches an oily consistency <term>Olität</term>. # Oil of Saturn of the Philosophers.
## The liquid gold of the wise.

Note. The watery distillate <note>original: "Phlegma"</note> is effective against epilepsy; one can use the volatile spirit in place of the spirit of wine <note>alcohol</note> for the <term>philosophical vitriol</term> <note>a metallic salt, likely iron sulfate in this context, considered a primary matter for the Great Work</note>.

Note. If the vitriol is driven for a long time by violent fire, all the thick salt passes over like a butter <note>a thick, oily substance resulting from distillation</note>, and the residue is a "dead earth" <note>the exhausted mineral remains</note> without salt.

Note. Batsdorff’s *Glowing Coals* <note>referring to the alchemical treatise "Filum Ariadne" by Heinrich von Batsdorff</note> and Basil Valentine’s <note>a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> philosophical gold.

Now when the spirit or gold is rectified, it should be coagulated again with <term>Mars</term> <note>the alchemical name for Iron</note> and become vitriol; *the solution of one is the coagulation of the other, and vice versa* <note>original Latin: "Unius Solutio est alterius coagulatio et e contra"</note>. Such a coagulated and dissolved substance is the Vitriol of Iron or the Vitriol of the Philosophers, in which the knowledge of all things is hidden; and this is the second rotation.

1. Outside of the One—which Basil kept secret—is Iron.

Rotation 2. Is the oil of native vitriol brought back into vitriol by means of Iron.

Note. Crystals or Vitriol of Iron sublimated with <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride</note>, the sal ammoniac leached out, the saffron-colored powder <note>original: "Crocum"</note> dried, the essence extracted with spirit of wine and brought to a powder, destroys common gold and turns it into powder.

A Proof. Of the Vitriol of Iron, to see if it contains philosophical mercury, or if the oil of vitriol was good or not.

Take 1 part of Vitriol of Iron, 2 parts of <term>Aqua Regia</term> <note>a powerful acid mixture capable of dissolving gold</note> made from spirit of nitre <note>nitric acid</note> and sal ammoniac; dissolve...

### Rotation 3, or how the diamond-essence and ruby-essence are brought forth from this Vitriol of Iron, and how the second internal purification takes place:

Grind 1 pound of Vitriol of Iron <note>original: "Vitriol. Martis"; iron sulfate</note> with 3 <term>Loth</term> <note>a historical German unit of weight, approximately 15 grams or half an ounce</note> of finely ground tartar <note>original: "Weinstein"</note> together; put it into a flask. Furthermore, add <note>original: "addiret"</note> 24 Loth of volatile salt of urine <note>original: "Sal volatile urinæ"; ammonium carbonate</note>, or a highly rectified spirit of urine <note>original: "Spiritum rectificatissimum urinæ"</note>, and 12, 14, or at most 16 Loth of highly rectified spirit of wine <note>original: "Spirit. vini rectificatissimi"; concentrated alcohol</note>. Seal <note>original: "lutiret"; to seal a joint with clay or paste</note> a still-head <note>original: "Helm"</note> and receiver <note>original: "Recipientz"</note> and distill gradually <note>original: "gradatim"</note> until a white mist begins to rise, or a sublimate is felt to be forming. In this way, everything that should and must pass over will do so in streaks <note>original: "Striis"</note> and flow down like a fatty oil.

This is the mercurial spirit, which rises by ascending <note>original: "ascendendo"</note> through the mediation of the tartar and the microcosmic spirit <note>original: "mediante Tartaro & Spirit. microcosmico"; "microcosmic spirit" refers to human-derived substances like urine, considered a reflection of the universe</note> in the form of a subtle viscous matter <note>original: "Viscosa"</note>; sometimes it floats upon the liquid <note>original: "Liquore"</note> like a fat.

Once the volatile mercury <note>original: "Volatile Mercuriale"</note> has come over, immediately lift the glass slightly out of the sand <note>referring to a sand bath used for heating</note>.

Thus, this small portion of 4 or 6 Loth has the capacity to gradually awaken a hundred pounds or more of the "dead" spirit.

In the aforementioned spirit or double salt, metals dissolve like ice; it is increased infinitely through the "common mercury"—namely, the salt just described, prepared from common vitriol.

### Preparation of Philosophical Mercury from the Ore of Saturn
<note>In the symbolic language of alchemy, "Saturn" refers to the metal lead.</note>

Take <note>original: Rec., an abbreviation for the Latin Recipe</note> our now well-known ore, which can be found in all places. Grind it as fine as flour upon a marble stone, as much as you wish. Place this into a large flask or vial, and pour distilled wine vinegar over it to a depth of about two fingers' breadth. Let it stand in very gentle heat for 3 or 4 days, well sealed at the top so that the scent does not escape. The vinegar will then be colored a beautiful yellow, and this pouring-over must be done repeatedly until the vinegar no longer tastes sweet.

### Preparation of Gold.

Take pure gold and make an amalgam <note>a soft, putty-like alloy of mercury and another metal</note> with crude mercury <note>original: Mercur. crud.</note> as the goldsmiths do. Afterward, take 1 loth <note>a historical German unit of weight, approximately 15 grams</note> of vitriol <note>a sulfate mineral</note>, 2 loth of sublimated sal ammoniac <note>original: Salmiac; ammonium chloride</note>, 1 loth of saltpeter, and 3 loth of sulfur <note>original: Schwefel</note>, all powdered together. Then take 1 pound of Tutty of Alexandria <term>Tutia Alexandrina; an impure form of zinc oxide often found in the chimneys of smelting furnaces</term>; calcine it <note>heat it until it glows</note> 12 times, quenching it each time in oil of tartar <note>original: Oleo Tartari; a concentrated solution of potassium carbonate</note>, and finally 5 times in oil of alum. Do it in this way:

Rub calcined alum with oil of tartar very cleanly and let it dissolve <note>original: solviren</note> in a damp cellar. Afterward, take the tutty which you quenched as stated above; place it in a crucible <note>original: Tiegel</note> well-sealed with luting <note>original: verlutiret; a clay or paste used to make a vessel airtight</note>, and place it in a wind-furnace <note>original: Wind-Ofen; a furnace that uses a bellows or natural draft to reach high temperatures</note> that is not blowing too strongly for one day. Let it remain inside, and thus it is fixed <note>original: fix; rendered stable and non-volatile under heat</note>. Then take 4 loth of sublimated sal ammoniac that has been sublimated through vitriol, calcined verdigris <note>original: Grünspahn</note>, 2 loth of Tutty of Alexandria, and 1 loth of burnt copper <term>Ælustum; likely Aes Ustum, a traditional term for copper roasted with sulfur</term>. Rub all of these together well and divide into 4 parts. Afterward, melt the previously mentioned prepared gold and silver together and throw the 4 parts of the powder into it one by one, stirring well and covering with glowing coals. Afterward, pour it out, and you will have gold of 20 degrees <note>likely referring to the fineness or "grade" of the resulting gold alloy</note>.

...tie the bladders tightly <note>Animal bladders were commonly used as airtight seals or covers for jars.</note> so that the <term>spirits|volatile essences or vapors</term> cannot evaporate. Now you have a stone jug ready, or a square glass, in which—as already mentioned—the urine must be saved. Into this glass, add the crushed powder according to the age of the patient: for a child, 1 to 2 <term>Loth|a historical unit of weight, approximately 15 grams or half an ounce</term>; for a sixteen-year-old, 3 to 4; and for an elderly person, 5 to 6 Loth. This addition must be done in the evening after the sun has set; then tie it shut and shake it well, then set it in a place that is neither too warm nor too cold until morning. During this time, the patient remains in bed, covered with a standard blanket. Then shake the glass well with the urine and powder and place it on the stove in warm sand, or in a bowl with warm water. Once this has stood for a quarter of an hour, the patient will sweat profusely <note>original: "horribel," meaning an intense or tremendous amount</note>, yet without fatigue. You may leave the glass there as long as you like; as long as the glass remains in the heat, the patient will sweat. If they wish to stop sweating, the glass is moved to a temperate place, and finally to a somewhat colder one, and thus the sweating is over. This urine—meaning the glass used for sweating—can be used for 3 days. Afterward, as mentioned, one must put new <term>species|a medicinal mixture of herbs or ingredients</term> and urine into the glass and proceed further as described. The contents of the glass may be poured into running water or buried in the earth.

Color as the <term>sulfur</term><note>In alchemical theory, 'sulfur' refers to the principle of combustibility and color, not just the chemical element.</note> in <term>spirit of wine</term><note>original: "Spiritum vini"; high-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>, also the salt, so that they both swim separately within—the former on top with its color, the latter below as a liquid—as you may then clearly see in the <term>solution</term><note>original: "solutione".</note>. Then take and pour off the <term>spirit of wine</term> separately from the <term>residue</term><note>original: "Remanentia".</note>. Collect the <term>spirit of wine</term> in which the color or sulfur resides separately; and the other in which the salt resides also separately. After that, draw off the <term>spirit of wine</term> from the salt. Onto this salt, pour <term>turpentine vinegar with spirit of wine</term><note>original: "Acetum Terebinthinae cum Spiritu vini".</note>, then draw it off; thus, finally, the salt also passes over through the <term>retort</term><note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note> in the <term>form of an oil</term><note>original: "forma Olei".</note>. <term>Digest</term><note>To soften or extract by gentle heat.</note> the sulfur with its <term>spirit of wine</term>, then draw off the <term>spirit of wine</term> until it reaches an oily consistency. Thus you see how the <term>turpentine vinegar</term> and <term>spirit of wine</term> beautifully separate the <term>three principles</term><note>original: "tria Principia"; the Paracelsian triad of Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt believed to compose all matter.</note> of all things. This turpentine vinegar serves well for fainting spells; let the patient smell it, and smear the pulses with it. **Also:** Whoever has dim vision should often smell the vinegar and hold his eyes over it; it clears the sight.

## To make porcelain ware as beautiful as those from China.

Take 10 pounds of sand which does not turn red in the fire but remains white, whether it be made from <term>pebbles</term><note>original: "Kißlingen"; likely flint or quartz pebbles.</note> or stones that do not burn to lime. Take 100 pounds of lead and 30 pounds of tin; let the lead be <term>reverberated</term><note>Heated in a furnace where the flame is reflected down onto the material.</note> until it is a beautiful red (which happens in one hour). To this, mix your tin, which has been burnt to <term>calx</term><note>original: "Kalg"; a powder or ash produced by heating a metal.</note> by itself in closed crucibles in a potter's oven. Then take 10 pounds of the mentioned sand and 12 pounds of the mixed <term>calx</term>, a seventh part and tin as mentioned, and also 6 pounds of sea salt; this makes 28 pounds in total. Mix it well and place it together in a crucible, which should not be too wide or flat so that the fire passes through uniformly. Apply fire gradually and increase
